# Every Process In Making A Picture

From somebody having an idea to somebody being paid for it, as a list of named processes with the decision inside each one.

This is the map SLATE builds from. It is deliberately written as processes rather than as stages, because a stage is a label and a process is a thing a person does that could have gone another way.

## What is in here

10 domains, 613 named processes, 257 option spaces. Every process says who does it, what it is, **what gets decided** — which is the part that becomes a choice in SLATE — what the engine behind it would have to hold, how long it takes, and how television differs from film.

An **option space** is a list of everything a given choice can be. They are the answer to wanting every option at hand: not a dropdown of names, but the real set, each entry of which has to carry a consequence before it earns its place.

### The domains

1. **Development: idea to greenlight (film and television), including rights, pitching, script development, packaging, financing plans, and the actual yes** — 61 processes, 20 option spaces
2. **The screenplay itself** — 64 processes, 23 option spaces
3. **Genre, format, rating and everything a picture IS** — 57 processes, 39 option spaces
4. **Characters, roles and arcs** — 58 processes, 26 option spaces
5. **Script breakdown and scheduling** — 63 processes, 27 option spaces
6. **Budgeting, financing and the bond** — 64 processes, 21 option spaces
7. **Casting, end to end** — 58 processes, 15 option spaces
8. **Every crew position, every department** — 58 processes, 32 option spaces
9. **Production design, art and construction** — 63 processes, 24 option spaces
10. **Costume, hair, makeup and prosthetics (film and television)** — 67 processes, 30 option spaces

---

## 1. Development: idea to greenlight (film and television), including rights, pitching, script development, packaging, financing plans, and the actual yes

### Idea origination and slate logging

A premise is fixed as arena + protagonist + engine + irony, given an origin type (original, underlying IP, life rights, format, remake, sequel), and entered on a company's slate.

- **Who.** Writer (WGA member); Creative Executive ("CE"); Producer
- **Decides.** Which of the three people generates it (actor's passion project, mother's company acquisition, father's original), the arena and tone, and whether it stays writer-owned spec or becomes a company-commissioned asset.
- **Engine.** Project record: origin_type, arena/tone tags, comps set, ownership (writer-owned vs work-for-hire), chain_of_title state, WGA-covered flag, and a hidden quality latent revealed only through reads, notes and tests — never shown as a number.
- **Takes.** 1 day to 3 weeks
- **Television.** TV needs a repeatable story engine (a machine that makes episode 47), a franchise statement and a character ensemble; a film premise with no weekly generator dies in the room.

### Comparables and window analysis

Three to eight comparable titles are pulled with budget, domestic/international gross or viewership, genre, rating and release window, to argue what this costs and earns.

- **Who.** Development Executive; Studio Strategy/Finance analyst; Producer
- **Decides.** Which comps to cite (flattering vs defensible), what budget tier to aim at, target rating (PG-13 vs R changes audience math), and what season to target.
- **Engine.** A title database with budget, P&A, theatrical/PVOD/TV revenue by window, or streaming completion/subscriber-share; a regression from comps to an ultimates projection; a credibility check that punishes cherry-picked comps at greenlight.
- **Takes.** 2 days to 2 weeks
- **Television.** Comps are cost-per-episode, retention and completion rate against the buyer's own library, not box office; a comp on a rival service can be argued away as "not our audience".

### IP scouting and submission intake

Galleys, manuscripts, proposals, articles, podcasts, comics, formats and stage plays are read pre-publication and flagged. Scouts work on retainer for studios and international clients.

- **Who.** Book Scout (NY/London); Story Editor; Head of Development; Literary Agent (book-to-film dept)
- **Decides.** Scout retainer budget and territories, which categories to cover, how early to jump (proposal vs finished manuscript vs bestseller list).
- **Engine.** A rolling IP market: items with publication date, heat, price expectation, competing bidders, and an exclusivity clock. Early reads cost money and give information asymmetry; late reads face auction prices.
- **Takes.** Continuous; 24-72 hours per hot manuscript
- **Television.** Series buyers want a world with many stories and a returning cast; a tight novel is a film, a sprawling one is a limited series, a series of novels is a returning series.

### Rights availability and chain-of-title search

Confirms who actually controls the rights: author vs publisher vs estate vs prior optionee, territory splits, reversions, and any encumbrance from a prior film version.

- **Who.** Entertainment attorney; Business Affairs; rights clearance researcher
- **Decides.** Whether to proceed, whether to buy out a lapsed prior option, whether to accept split or limited rights (e.g. no sequel, no stage).
- **Engine.** Per-IP rights ledger: rights holder graph, granted/reserved bundles (film, TV, stage, publication, merchandising, radio, interactive, remake, sequel, prequel), territory, term, and lapse dates. A defective chain blocks financing and E&O later, not at the moment of the mistake.
- **Takes.** 1 to 6 weeks
- **Television.** Format rights and "series based on" rights are separate grants; a film option that doesn't carve out television leaves the TV version unmakeable.

### Life rights negotiation

An agreement for a living person's cooperation, consultation, name/likeness and a release from defamation and privacy claims; often exclusive for a term.

- **Who.** Producer; entertainment attorney; the subject and their counsel
- **Decides.** Option fee and purchase price, consultation rights, approval over depiction, whether family members are included, exclusivity length, credit ("Consulting Producer").
- **Engine.** Life rights are not copyright: model the difference. Without the deal the project is still legally possible but carries litigation and E&O risk, loses cooperation (access to detail, endorsement, publicity), and rivals can make a competing unauthorized version.
- **Takes.** 3 weeks to 9 months
- **Television.** Docuseries and limited series buy the same rights plus archive and interview participation; a returning series needs ongoing consultation, not a one-time release.

### Shopping agreement

A short, usually unpaid exclusive right to take the material to buyers for a defined period, with an agreed deal shape if a buyer bites. Not an option: no rights transfer.

- **Who.** Producer; writer or rights holder; their attorney
- **Decides.** Term (typically 3-12 months), buyer list attached to the agreement, whether the producer is guaranteed attachment, whether the writer can accept a deal that cuts the producer out.
- **Engine.** State machine distinct from option: no purchase price paid, no ability to hire another writer, and the material is not "controlled" for financing purposes. Buyers can and do go around a shopping agreement to the rights holder.
- **Takes.** Days to negotiate; 3-12 month term
- **Television.** Very common for TV, where a producer attaches and shops to networks/streamers; a studio will usually insist on converting to an option before a script order.

### Option agreement negotiation

Exclusive right to buy the material within a term, for a fee applicable against the purchase price. Under the WGA MBA, optioning literary material from a "professional writer" costs at least 10% of the applicable theatrical minimum for up to 18 months, and 10% more per renewal period; in television, 5% of minimum for the first 180 days and 10% for each further 180 days.

- **Who.** Business Affairs executive; producer; literary agent; entertainment attorney
- **Decides.** Fee, term, number and price of renewals, purchase price (fixed, or commonly 2-3% of budget with floor and ceiling), bonuses, sequel/remake passive payments, backend points, credit, reversion terms, and whether the fee is applicable or non-applicable.
- **Engine.** Option object with term clock, renewal ledger, applicable/non-applicable flag, purchase price formula bound to the live budget number, and set-up/production bonuses. When the clock expires unexercised, rights snap back and any drafts commissioned become dead weight.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks to paper; 12-18 month typical term
- **Television.** TV options run in 180-day beats to match pitch cycles; if/come structures replace the option entirely for writer-created series.

### Option exercise, lapse and reversion

On exercise the purchase price is paid and copyright assigns. On lapse, all rights revert; any material created under the option (drafts, bible) is typically owned by the company but unusable without the underlying rights.

- **Who.** Business Affairs; producer; rights holder's counsel
- **Decides.** Exercise now, renew, renegotiate an extension for equity or a bigger purchase price, or let it drop and lose sunk development cost.
- **Engine.** Two coupled ledgers: rights state and sunk cost. Exercise usually triggers only on start of principal photography or on greenlight; a lapse mid-financing kills a deal, so the option clock must be a live constraint that the financing timeline can crash into.
- **Takes.** Instant on payment; renewal decision at term end
- **Television.** Reversion in TV frequently includes a "frozen" period where the writer can't re-set the project for 6-12 months.

### Copyright registration and WGA Registry deposit

Registering the script with the U.S. Copyright Office (statutory damages and attorney's fees available for timely registration) and/or depositing with the WGAW Registry ($20 public, $10 WGA members, five-year term).

- **Who.** Writer; entertainment attorney; paralegal
- **Decides.** Whether to register at all, at which draft, and whether to register in the writer's name or the company's (work-for-hire).
- **Engine.** Cheap, slow paperwork that only matters when a plagiarism/idea-theft claim fires. Model claim probability from pitch exposure (every buyer meeting increases the surface), and make registration the mitigant.
- **Takes.** Minutes to file; Copyright Office processing months
- **Television.** Bibles and formats are registrable and are the thing most often copied; format claims are the common TV dispute.

### Title clearance and script clearance report

Checks the title against registered/prior titles and runs a script clearance report on names, businesses, phone numbers, addresses, logos, trademarks and songs mentioned.

- **Who.** Clearance company (e.g. a research service); production counsel; MPA Title Registration Bureau participant
- **Decides.** Keep or change the title, and how many flagged elements to change now versus argue with the E&O carrier later.
- **Engine.** Title registry with conflict distances and arbitration; per-script list of flagged items with a change cost and a residual risk. Feeds E&O premium and marketing.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks
- **Television.** Series titles must clear for the series and for episodes; long-running shows re-clear each season.

### Submission policy and release forms

Unsolicited material is refused or accepted only with a signed submission release; represented submissions come through agents/managers and get logged.

- **Who.** Story Department coordinator; Legal; assistants
- **Decides.** Whether the mother's company accepts unagented material at all — a real strategy choice trading discovery upside against idea-theft litigation exposure.
- **Engine.** Two intake pipes with different distributions: agented flow is smaller, higher median, gated by relationships; open flow is huge, lower median, occasional outlier, and carries lawsuit risk if a similar project later ships.
- **Takes.** Immediate; log within 24 hours
- **Television.** Same, but TV also fields "take" submissions on studio-owned IP from staffed writers.

### Logline authoring

One or two sentences: protagonist, inciting event, goal, obstacle, irony. It is the unit that travels — on the tracking board, in the trades, in the buyer's weekend email.

- **Who.** Writer; Creative Executive; agent
- **Decides.** Which angle to lead with; whether to sell genre, star vehicle or theme; and the title that rides with it.
- **Engine.** Loglines must be assembled from real project fields, not written text, so a change to protagonist/obstacle changes the buyer's read. Score them against each buyer's appetite vector; the same logline should perform differently at different companies.
- **Takes.** 1 hour to several days of iteration
- **Television.** A TV logline must include the engine ("...each week") and the franchise; "closed-ended" loglines get bounced.

### One-pager and sell sheet

A single page: title, logline, tone/comps, brief story, the engine (TV), attachments, budget tier and territory strategy. It is what gets forwarded internally.

- **Who.** Producer; Development Executive; the writer
- **Decides.** What to disclose (budget range, attachments), whether to name comps, and whether to send it before a meeting or leave it behind after.
- **Engine.** A document object composed from project state; a stale one-pager (attachment gone, budget moved) actively damages credibility if circulated. Track who received which version and when.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days
- **Television.** Adds the season-one arc, the ensemble, and episode-count/format (8 x 60, 10 x 30).

### Treatment

Prose telling of the whole story in present tense, typically 5-30 pages, sometimes 60+ for an epic. Delivery of an original treatment is a payable step (theatrical high-budget original treatment installment $69,228 for 5/2/26-5/1/27).

- **Who.** Writer (paid under WGA "Story or Treatment" / "Original Story or Treatment" minimums)
- **Decides.** Length and detail, whether to include dialogue snippets, whether the company buys treatment-first (cheaper, slower) or goes straight to script.
- **Engine.** A step with money, a clock and an outcome that alters the script's later shape. If the treatment can be approved, noted or rejected without changing anything downstream, it is decoration — the accepted treatment must constrain the beats the draft can contain.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks
- **Television.** Rare as a paid TV step; TV pays for story, format, bible and teleplay instead.

### Beat sheet and card board

The story reduced to 30-60 beats on index cards or a board (often act-structured), used to test structure before pages exist.

- **Who.** Writer; showrunner; development executive in a beat session
- **Decides.** Act breaks, midpoint, sequence order, which beats get cut when the page count is too high.
- **Engine.** Beats are objects with function tags (setup, turn, reversal, act-out) that later map to scenes, then to a schedule and budget. Move a beat to a new location and the script, the schedule and the day count must all move.
- **Takes.** 3 days to 3 weeks
- **Television.** TV beats the whole season on a board (one column per episode) before any script; the room does this collectively, and act-outs are mandatory for ad-supported outlets.

### Step outline

Scene-by-scene numbered list with slugline, who's in it, what changes. Often the last cheap place to fix structure.

- **Who.** Writer; Creative Executive
- **Decides.** How granular to go, and whether to get studio sign-off on the outline before writing (protects the writer, slows the process, invites notes early).
- **Engine.** Scene list with location, cast, day/night, int/ext — the same records that later become the breakdown. Approving an outline should lock a subset of scenes so later drafts show measurable drift from approval.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks
- **Television.** Every episode gets an outline that the studio and the network/streamer both approve before teleplay; that approval is a hard gate and a payment trigger.

### Format and series bible

The format is the show's shape and rules; the bible is the long document — world, engine, characters, season arc, episode ideas. Both are separately payable WGA steps: format minimum $20,000 (up 42% in the 2026 MBA), network prime time bible $72,431, non-network/non-prime bible $57,945, plus $7,243/$5,795 for each story line beyond six (5/2/26-5/1/27).

- **Who.** Creator; showrunner; development executive
- **Decides.** Whether to pay for a bible at all, how many story lines to include (each beyond six costs money), and how much of season two to reveal.
- **Engine.** Bible content must be readable by later systems: characters with arcs, story lines as reusable engine slots, and a season plan the room can execute. Story-line count is a paid quantity, so "more detail" has a real price.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks
- **Television.** This process is television-only; features have no equivalent paid step.

### Pilot script

The teleplay for episode one. WGA pilot minimum is 150% of the applicable story-and/or-teleplay minimum; network prime time 60-minute story & teleplay is $47,460 (5/2/26-5/1/27), so a one-hour pilot script floor is roughly $71,190. 10% of the first installment is advanced at commencement; a back-up script is 115%.

- **Who.** Writer/creator, employed by the studio or via if/come
- **Decides.** Premise pilot vs typical episode, cold open vs teaser, how much of the engine to demonstrate, running length, act count.
- **Engine.** Script object with act structure, page count, cast size, location count and set requirements — the inputs the pilot budget and schedule read. Pay the real installment structure: story 30% of agreed comp, first draft to 90% of minimum, balance on final draft.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks including notes rounds
- **Television.** Entirely TV. A film has no equivalent of a script that must simultaneously be a movie, a sales tool, and a promise about 100 more hours.

### Spec screenplay

A finished screenplay written without a buyer, owned by the writer, sold complete. Sale to a signatory must pay at least the WGA purchase minimum for a professional writer ($126,899 high-budget original screenplay excluding treatment, 5/2/26-5/1/27).

- **Who.** Writer, unemployed and unpaid, on their own material
- **Decides.** Genre, budget tier written into the pages, page count, and when to release it to the market.
- **Engine.** Writer's time is the cost; the asset is fully owned. Model the writer's finances during the write (no income), the calendar (specs launch Monday, not the week of a holiday or a market), and the ownership advantage at sale versus a work-for-hire assignment.
- **Takes.** 6 weeks to 2 years
- **Television.** A TV spec is a sample, not a sale — spec pilots almost never sell but get their writer staffed or an overall deal; a spec of an existing show is a writing sample only.

### Format and page discipline

US screenplay format: Courier 12pt, 1.5" left margin, roughly one page per minute. Feature 90-120 pages; half-hour teleplay 22-35; one-hour 45-65.

- **Who.** Writer; script coordinator; assistant
- **Decides.** Software (Final Draft, Fade In, Highland, WriterDuet), page target, and whether to cheat margins to hit a page count.
- **Engine.** Page count must be computed from actual content (dialogue vs action density changes the ratio), and it drives the schedule estimate and the WGA length category, which sets the minimum fee. Cheating format should be detectable by a line producer's estimate diverging from page count.
- **Takes.** Ongoing
- **Television.** Broadcast hours run about 42-44 minutes of content in five or six acts with act-outs; streaming hours run 45-70 with no act breaks. Same "one hour", different scripts.

### Script coverage

A reader produces logline, 1-2 page synopsis, comments, a grid (premise, structure, characterization, dialogue, setting) with excellent/good/fair/poor, and a verdict: recommend, consider, pass — separately for the script and for the writer.

- **Who.** Story Analyst (IATSE Local 700 at the majors; freelance readers elsewhere)
- **Decides.** Whether to use in-house union analysts or cheap freelancers, how many reads before a decision, and whether to overrule bad coverage on relationship grounds.
- **Engine.** Readers are agents with taste vectors, fatigue, genre bias and speed. The same script gets different coverage from different readers; coverage is written into the studio's permanent story files, so a pass follows the script around town.
- **Takes.** 3-6 hours per script; 24-72 hour turnaround
- **Television.** TV coverage weights the engine, character ensemble and "can it run 100 episodes" over structure; pilots also get a "series potential" note.

### Tracking boards and town read

Loglines, coverage, sale news and staffing intel circulate on private tracking boards and by text hours before the trades. Heat is manufactured here.

- **Who.** Assistants and junior executives; agency trainees; managers
- **Decides.** Whether to leak, to whom, and when; whether to deny a project is going out; whether to plant a competing bid.
- **Engine.** An information-diffusion layer: each project has visibility, distortion and heat that spread on a relationship graph. Buyers respond to perceived competition, so heat measurably changes price — and false heat is detectable and burns the leaker's credibility.
- **Takes.** Hours
- **Television.** TV tracking peaks around pitch season and staffing season; feature tracking peaks on Mondays when specs go out.

### Spec going out and the bid

The script goes to a chosen buyer list at an agreed hour. Buyers read overnight; offers come as bids, preempts (a high offer to stop the process) or passes. Since July 1 2022, franchised agents may not take packaging fees on WGA-covered projects and work on 10% commission only.

- **Who.** Literary agent (WGA-franchised, 10% commission); manager; entertainment attorney
- **Decides.** Buyer list and its size, whether to go wide or targeted, timing, whether to accept a preempt, and floor price.
- **Engine.** An auction with heterogeneous bidders — each buyer has slate needs, appetite, a president's taste, cash, and a read time. Preempt logic, agency relationships and the writer's quote all set price. No package fee exists in the current era; the agency's incentive is now purely commission on the writer's fee.
- **Takes.** 24-96 hours from send to close
- **Television.** Specs rarely sell in TV; the equivalent is an attachment-plus-pitch rollout to buyers.

### The take and the bake-off

For an open assignment on studio-owned IP, several writers meet, pitch their "take" (approach, tone, structure) and compete. Unpaid unless a company pays for a take under the MBA.

- **Who.** Writer; Creative Executive; Producer; studio Head of Development
- **Decides.** Whether to do the free take at all, how specific to be (specific ideas can be taken and given to another writer), and whether to bring artwork or a document.
- **Engine.** Bake-offs consume the writer's calendar with no fee and a win probability driven by fit, relationships and the executive's taste; losing takes still change the project because ideas leak into the hired writer's brief.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks prep; meetings over 2-4 weeks
- **Television.** TV's version is the "take" on an IP-based series, and the buyer may hire the take-giver as showrunner rather than as script writer.

### Pitch document, look book, sizzle

Visual materials: mood boards, character art, tone reels cut from existing footage, or a shot proof-of-concept. Increasingly expected for high-concept and genre.

- **Who.** Producer; creator; graphic designer; editor cutting a sizzle
- **Decides.** Spend level (a designed deck vs a shot proof-of-concept costing tens of thousands), whether to license music for the sizzle, and whether visuals help or over-define.
- **Engine.** Real money out of the development budget, with a measurable effect on buyer conviction for visual-heavy concepts and near-zero effect on character dramas. Sizzles built from other films' footage cannot be used publicly — model the clearance wall.
- **Takes.** 1-6 weeks
- **Television.** TV pitch decks are near-mandatory at streamers; features rely more on the script itself.

### Buyer clearance and pitch rollout

Before pitching, the buyer list is cleared (avoiding conflicts with in-house projects and overall-deal obligations), then meetings are stacked into two to four days.

- **Who.** Agent; producer's assistant; studio's TV distribution executive
- **Decides.** Order of buyers (favourite first or last), how many rooms, whether to hold one buyer in reserve, and which of the three playable people takes the meeting.
- **Engine.** Each buyer has a conflict list, an appetite window and a calendar; a clash with a similar in-house project produces an instant pass regardless of quality. Pitch order matters because a first sale ends the rollout.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks to schedule; 2-4 days to execute
- **Television.** Under an overall deal the writer must take a TV project to the home studio first; features have no such first-position obligation unless contracted.

### The pitch meeting

Fifteen to forty-five minutes, in the room or on video: the hook, why me/why now, the world, the characters, the pilot story, the season, the engine, the seasons after, comps, and the ask.

- **Who.** Creator/writer pitching; producer; studio executive; network/streamer executive and their team
- **Decides.** Verbal-only or with materials, length, how much of season two to reveal, who speaks when, whether to take notes and re-pitch, and what to say when asked "who plays this?"
- **Engine.** A scored interaction, not a cutscene: buyer executives have taste vectors, mandates, current-slate holes, and a hierarchy (the CE loved it but the president decides). Model interruption, the killer question, and the "we're doing something similar" wall. Outcomes: buy in the room, pass, pass-with-invite, or steal-adjacent.
- **Takes.** 20-45 minutes per room
- **Television.** TV pitches are the industry's main sale mechanism and must survive a second, larger room; feature pitches usually happen only for assignments or with an attached star.

### If/come deal

The studio and writer agree terms in advance, but nothing is paid unless a network/streamer commits. From January 1 2027 under the 2026 MBA, an if/come pilot agreement may not make the writer exclusive and puts the company in no position until it has paid for the first pilot-script step or purchased the script.

- **Who.** Business Affairs (studio); writer's attorney and agent
- **Decides.** Whether to accept if/come rather than a paid development deal, how many buyers may be approached, and how long the studio's window runs.
- **Engine.** A contingent contract object: terms locked, money conditional, exclusivity now legally barred pre-payment. That single rule change alters the whole board — writers can shop the same idea to multiple studios and the studio must pay to lock it.
- **Takes.** Papering 2-6 weeks; window 3-12 months
- **Television.** Television-only structure.

### Development deal papering

Deal memo covering the major points, then long-form. The MBA requires delivery of a contract or deal memo generally within 10-12 days of agreement on major deal points, and payment of at least 50% of a step's fee on commencement for flat-deal writers at twice minimum or less.

- **Who.** Business Affairs executive; entertainment attorney; WGA signatory administrator
- **Decides.** Fee per step, guaranteed vs optional steps, bonuses (production bonus, sole credit bonus, box office bumps), backend definition, credit, and sequel/spin-off passive payments.
- **Engine.** Contract objects with enforceable clauses: payment triggers, guarantees, options, exclusivity, and the WGA floors. Interest at 1.5%/month accrues on late payment and Guild claims follow — deadbeat producers must actually suffer.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks
- **Television.** TV deals add episodic fees, per-episode script fees, term/overall commitments, and title (Co-Producer through Executive Producer) with a weekly rate attached.

### Overall, first-look, pod and blind script deals

Overall deal: exclusive services for a term, paid against project fees, with the studio owning what's created. First-look: the studio sees it first and can pass. Pod/housekeeping: office and staff at the studio. Blind script: a paid pilot script with no idea attached yet. The 2026 MBA raises the Health Fund contribution cap on overall deals from $275,000 to $325,000 in year one, reaching $400,000 by year three.

- **Who.** Studio/streamer Business Affairs; showrunner or producer; agent
- **Decides.** For the mother: whether to take a first-look with a studio (money and overhead, loss of freedom) or stay independent. For the father: whether to sign an overall and where.
- **Engine.** Deal objects with term, annual guarantee, overhead payment, project-fee applicability, exclusivity carve-outs, and productivity expectations. Model the studio's ROI: overall deals must be evaluated against what the producer actually delivered, and get renewed or dropped accordingly.
- **Takes.** 1-4 months to negotiate; 2-5 year terms
- **Television.** Overall deals are overwhelmingly a TV instrument; feature producers get first-look and housekeeping deals instead.

### Public development funding application

Applications to public funds: BFI NETWORK Early Development (£3,000-£5,000 for a treatment, with a second stage for a first draft), Screen Ireland Irish Feature Film Development (up to €75,000, or €125,000 with third-party match), Screen Australia Narrative Content Development ($10,000-$50,000 per application), Telefilm, Icelandic Film Centre screenwriting and development grants.

- **Who.** Producer; development executive; funding body assessor
- **Decides.** Which territory to develop in, which fund and tier, what to promise in the application, and how to structure the team to qualify.
- **Engine.** Each fund has eligibility rules (nationality, track record, attachment requirements), an assessment calendar with deadlines and decision dates, a hit rate, and repayment terms (development loans typically repayable on the first day of principal photography, often with a premium — estimate). Nationality tests bind later co-production choices.
- **Takes.** 6 weeks to 6 months per round
- **Television.** Broadcaster-linked funds require a letter of interest from a broadcaster; TV development money often comes from the commissioner instead of a fund.

### Commissioning a writer (step deal design)

Structuring the writing assignment as steps: story/treatment, first draft, rewrite, polish — each with a fee and a delivery date. Under the 2026 MBA, hiring a writer for a first draft at 225% or less of minimum (up from 200%) obliges the company to employ them for a rewrite at no less than the rewrite minimum.

- **Who.** Business Affairs; producer; the writer's agent
- **Decides.** Number of guaranteed steps versus optional steps, fee level relative to minimum (which triggers the guaranteed second step), delivery dates and the reading period between steps.
- **Engine.** Steps as first-class objects with money, dates, and legal constraints; the 225% threshold must actually be computed against the live minimum table so that a cheap deal automatically buys a second step. Cheating this is a Guild claim.
- **Takes.** Deal 2-6 weeks; the steps themselves run months
- **Television.** TV commissions story, outline and teleplay per episode with approvals between each, and the writer is often also an employee under Article 14 with a weekly rate.

### Notes call and notes memo

After a draft is delivered, the company gives notes — a call, then a written memo. Notes are ordered by priority and typically include structural, character, tone and page-count notes.

- **Who.** Creative Executive; VP/SVP of Development; producer; the writer
- **Decides.** Which notes to take, which to push back on, which to fake-take, and whether to escalate to the person above the note-giver.
- **Engine.** Notes as objects with source, priority, and a specific target (a beat, a character, an act). Taking a note must change the script object; refusing must have a relationship and approval cost. Two notes can be mutually contradictory, and the engine must let that happen.
- **Takes.** 1-2 week turnaround for the memo; call is 45-90 minutes
- **Television.** TV notes come in a fixed order — studio notes first, then network/streamer, sometimes a second round from each — and are attached to hard approval gates.

### The note hierarchy and notes on notes

Notes pass down a chain and are re-interpreted at each level. "Notes on notes" happen when the studio filters the network's notes, or when a junior executive passes their boss's note with their own added.

- **Who.** Producer; studio executive; network/streamer executive; sometimes the star or director
- **Decides.** Whether to go around the note-giver, whether to ask for a call with the actual decision-maker, and whether to make a change to satisfy a note the writer thinks is wrong.
- **Engine.** Model the chain explicitly: each executive has authority, taste, and a boss. A note filtered through three people should arrive distorted. Satisfying the wrong level wastes a draft and a month.
- **Takes.** Adds 1-4 weeks per level
- **Television.** Film notes end with the studio; TV notes have a second sovereign (the buyer) whose note beats the studio's every time.

### Draft delivery, reading period, payment

Delivery triggers payment. The MBA requires best efforts to pay within 48 hours, never more than 7 days, and payment cannot be contingent on approval or on obtaining financing; late payment accrues 1.5% monthly interest. Under the 2026 MBA, only the named individual in the writer's contract can request rewrites or accept delivery on covered theatrical projects.

- **Who.** Writer; producer; Business Affairs; payroll
- **Decides.** When to deliver (early to trigger payment, late to keep working), and whether to accept informal "just one more pass" requests that are legally unpaid work.
- **Engine.** Delivery is a state transition with money attached and a reading period during which the writer owes nothing. Free-work requests must be refusable, and accepting them must produce a measurable cost in time and an unmeasured reputational gain.
- **Takes.** Payment 2-7 days; reading period typically 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** TV episodic payments follow story 30% / first draft to 90% of minimum / balance on final draft, per episode, with the same no-contingency rule.

### Draft numbering and revision convention

Development drafts are labelled by step and date ("First Draft, 3.14.26"), then "Second Draft", "Revised Draft", "Studio Draft", "Network Draft", "Production Draft". Once locked in production, revisions go on coloured pages in the standard order (white, blue, pink, yellow, green, goldenrod, buff, salmon, cherry, then double-white and onward).

- **Who.** Writer; script coordinator; development assistant
- **Decides.** What to call the draft handed over — labelling a rewrite a "polish" is a fee decision and a Guild issue; date-stamping is how disputes get resolved.
- **Engine.** Every draft is stored as an immutable versioned object with author, step name, date, page count and a diff against its parent. Credit arbitration, fee disputes and "who wrote what" all read those diffs, so drafts cannot be a single mutable text field.
- **Takes.** Instant on delivery
- **Television.** TV adds table draft, production draft, and per-episode colour revisions during a season; broadcast networks want a specific act-structured draft for their read.

### Rewrite, page-one rewrite, polish

Rewrite (theatrical high budget minimum $46,152), polish ($23,077), and the newly codified page-one rewrite ($57,500 high budget, $31,500 low budget for contracts on or after 5/2/26 — the minimum applies only if the contract says "page-one rewrite").

- **Who.** A new or the same writer; producer; studio executive
- **Decides.** Whether to rewrite with the original writer or hire a new one, what to call the step, and how many passes to fund before deciding the script is unfixable.
- **Engine.** Each rewrite consumes budget and calendar and changes the script's latent quality by an amount driven by the writer's fit for the specific problem — an action specialist should not fix a dialogue problem. Diminishing returns and drift from the original voice must be modelled.
- **Takes.** 2-10 weeks per pass
- **Television.** TV rewrites are done by the showrunner over the staff writer's draft as a matter of routine and are usually unpaid extra work by the showrunner, not a separate hire.

### Writer replacement and hand-off

A writer is let go and another hired, sometimes without the first being told promptly. The new writer receives the prior drafts and the notes history.

- **Who.** Studio/network executive; producer; both agents
- **Decides.** When to change writers, whether to tell the first writer, which prior drafts to pass on, and whether to bring a specialist (dialogue, action, comedy punch-up, structure).
- **Engine.** A writer chain per project with drafts attributed; each hand-off costs money and 4-12 weeks and injects the new writer's voice while retaining measurable material from the old — which later drives arbitration. Reputation effects on both writers and on the executive who keeps churning.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks per cycle
- **Television.** Replacing a creator on their own pilot usually means bringing in an experienced showrunner over them, which triggers a credit and control fight rather than a clean replacement.

### Credit determination and WGA arbitration

The company sends a Notice of Tentative Writing Credits; if a participating writer protests, or automatically when a production executive claims credit, the Guild runs an anonymous arbitration on the drafts. Outcomes: "Written by", "Screenplay by", "Story by", "Screen Story by", "Adaptation by". Subsequent writers must clear a substantial contribution threshold (customarily one-third, higher on original material — estimate; the Manual governs).

- **Who.** Company (proposes credits); WGA Credits Department; three anonymous arbiters; Screen Credits Manual
- **Decides.** Nothing, for the player — that is the point. The player decides only whether to protest, and what drafts to submit as evidence.
- **Engine.** An arbitration subsystem that reads the stored draft chain and applies threshold rules with imperfect, anonymous arbiters. Credit then drives separated rights, residuals, bonuses, the writer's quote and future hireability — so it must be a system, not a label.
- **Takes.** Notice period days; arbitration 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** TV determines "Created by" and "Developed by", which carry separated rights and series sequel payments ($5,109 per 60-minute episode, 5/2/26-5/1/27) for the life of the show.

### Separated rights and reserved rights

A writer with separated rights on a theatrical film keeps publication and dramatic stage rights subject to holdbacks (the company gets a window — commonly two years post-release — to make a stage version before the right passes to the writer). In TV, separated rights attach to the "Created by" writer and carry series sequel payments and a right to the first re-write and first negotiation.

- **Who.** WGA; writer's attorney; studio Business Affairs
- **Decides.** For the buyer: whether to negotiate for those rights above scale. For the writer: whether to exploit the novelization or stage version.
- **Engine.** A rights bundle that survives the sale, with holdback clocks and downstream revenue (novelization, stage). A game that sells "all rights" in one transaction gets the writer's long-run economics wrong.
- **Takes.** Determined at credit; holdbacks run years
- **Television.** TV separated rights pay per episode forever, which is the single biggest wealth mechanism for a creator and must be modelled as an annuity.

### Development room (mini-room)

Three or more writers convened before a firm series commitment. Under the MBA a development room must have at least 3 writer-producers including the showrunner, each guaranteed at least 10 consecutive weeks, at premium weekly rates ($7,530 staff writer, $14,043 story editor/exec story editor, $15,379 writer-producer, 5/2/26-5/1/27); development-room pay cannot be credited against later episodic fees, and a guarantee of 20+ weeks makes it a regular room.

- **Who.** Showrunner; two or more writer-producers; studio
- **Decides.** Whether to open a room pre-order at all, how many writers, how long, who staffs it, and what it must produce (scripts, outlines, a season plan).
- **Engine.** A room object with cost per week per seat, a legal floor of three seats and ten weeks, and outputs (episode outlines, scripts, a series plan) that measurably speed and improve later production. The rule makes a cheap two-person room illegal — the floor must bind.
- **Takes.** 10-19 weeks (20+ reclassifies the room)
- **Television.** Television-only. Features have no analogue; this is the clearest proof that TV is not film with more episodes.

### Showrunner attachment

A writer with running experience is attached to a creator's project, or hired over it. Deal includes overall-deal buyouts, title, control, and "who has final cut of the room".

- **Who.** Studio executive; creator; agent; the showrunner candidate
- **Decides.** Whether to attach one (buyers often require it for a first-time creator), which one from a list, and how much control the creator keeps.
- **Engine.** Showrunner candidates with availability (blocked by overall deals and current shows), track record, genre fit, and a price. Attachment measurably raises buyer confidence and raises cost; the wrong showrunner degrades scripts and staff retention.
- **Takes.** 3-10 weeks
- **Television.** Television-only role; the film equivalent is attaching a producer or director.

### Director attachment

A list is built and cleared, the script goes out to directors, meetings are taken on their "take", then a letter of intent or a deal (development period, then pay-or-play on greenlight).

- **Who.** Producer; studio Head of Production; director's agent; the director
- **Decides.** List order, whether to offer to a name who will slow the project, whether to accept a director-driven rewrite, and whether to make them pay-or-play before financing closes.
- **Engine.** Directors have availability windows, quote, genre fit, budget ceiling buyers will accept, and a set of buyer/financier relationships that change what money is available. A pay-or-play director who leaves costs the full fee — model the sunk liability.
- **Takes.** 6 weeks to 12 months
- **Television.** TV attaches a pilot director (often an EP) whose look sets the series; on a returning series directors are episodic hires, not attachments.

### Cast attachment: offers, avails and LOIs

Availability is checked through the agent, then a hard offer with dates, money and role. A letter of intent says the actor intends to do it if the film is financed and shoots in a window — it binds almost nothing. Pay-or-play means the fee is owed whether or not the film is made.

- **Who.** Producer; casting director; talent agent; entertainment attorney
- **Decides.** Offer list and order, offer size, dates offered, whether to go pay-or-play, whether to accept an LOI as an "essential element" for financiers, and what producer credits or backend to give.
- **Engine.** Actor objects with quote, avails calendar, agent gatekeeping, script-read time, interest driven by role size/director/co-star, and competing offers. LOI, soft attachment, pay-or-play and signed contract must be distinct states with different values to a financier and different collapse probabilities.
- **Takes.** Avails check 24-72 hours; offer to answer 1-6 weeks; deal 2-8 weeks
- **Television.** TV attaches series regulars with multi-year option deals (customarily up to six or seven seasons) negotiated before the network test, and a star attachment can itself trigger a straight-to-series order.

### The actor's read-and-decide

Material arrives from the agent with a note on who's attached and what's offered. The actor reads, meets the director, and answers. Offers expire; two offers can overlap and conflict on dates.

- **Who.** The actor (the player); their agent, manager, lawyer and publicist
- **Decides.** Read or pass unread, take the meeting, ask for a rewrite of the part, negotiate quote, backend, billing, perks, producer credit, first or second position, and whether to attach to an unfinanced indie for less money.
- **Engine.** The actor's own calendar, quote history, representation quality, and taste. Attaching to a project should change that project's financing odds by a computed amount, and the actor's decision must trade a real bird-in-hand against a probability-weighted better job.
- **Takes.** Read 1-3 days; decision 3 days to 4 weeks
- **Television.** A series is a multi-year lock with an option ladder and exclusivity that blocks films; a film is a fixed window. The opportunity cost calculation is entirely different.

### Casting director engagement and test deals

A casting director is engaged in development to build lists, take temperature and, in TV, run pre-reads, callbacks, studio test and network test. Test option deals are fully negotiated before the actor tests.

- **Who.** Casting Director (CSA); network casting executive; Business Affairs
- **Decides.** Which casting director (their relationships determine which agents pick up), how early to hire them, and which names to put on the network's approved list.
- **Engine.** Casting director objects with agency relationships, genre reputation and fee. Test deals must be papered before the test, so the negotiation happens under time pressure with the actor's leverage at its lowest — model that ordering.
- **Takes.** CD engaged 4-12 weeks before pilot casting; test cycle 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** Network/studio test is a TV ritual with no film equivalent; multiple actors test on the same day, deals pre-signed, and the network picks.

### Packaging in the post-fee era

Assembling writer, director and cast from one or more agencies to raise a project's value. Since July 1 2022 franchised agencies cannot take packaging fees on WGA-covered projects and are limited to 10% commission and 20% ownership of affiliated production companies; the practice continues as attachment-building, not a fee product.

- **Who.** Agency (no longer paid a package fee); producer; studio; manager-producer
- **Decides.** Whether to build the package inside one agency (fast, conflicted) or across several, and what to give away in credits and backend to get attachments.
- **Engine.** Model agency incentives correctly for the current era: agents profit from client fees, not package fees, which changes who they push for and when they will let a client work cheap. A game that still pays 3/3/10 package fees is simulating 2018.
- **Takes.** 1-6 months
- **Television.** TV packages were the historic profit centre and are the specific thing eliminated; agency-affiliated studios (production affiliates) are the modern substitute.

### Budget top sheet and schedule estimate

From the script: a scene breakdown, a rough board and day count, then a budget top sheet in Movie Magic Budgeting — above-the-line, below-the-line, post, and contingency (commonly 10%).

- **Who.** Line Producer; UPM; 1st AD (for the board); production executive
- **Decides.** Shooting territory, day count, cast size cap, whether to write out expensive elements, and the budget tier the project is pitched at.
- **Engine.** The estimate must be computed from the actual scene list (locations, cast, night work, stunts, VFX counts), not typed in. Change a beat and the day count moves; the budget then moves the incentive, the buyer set and the greenlight math.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks for a development estimate
- **Television.** TV budgets a pilot plus a pattern budget per episode and an amortisation schedule for sets, stages and series-level costs; the pilot is always more expensive than the pattern.

### Financing plan and recoupment waterfall

A one-page financing plan naming every source — equity, pre-sales, tax credit, gap, soft money, deferrals — and the waterfall in which they recoup, with a CAMA appointing a collection agent.

- **Who.** Producer; film finance advisor; entertainment attorney; sales agent
- **Decides.** Mix of instruments, order of recoupment, corridor for talent, whether to take a co-production partner, and how much equity to keep.
- **Engine.** A real waterfall calculator with senior/mezzanine/equity tiers, interest, fees and corridors, tested against multiple revenue scenarios. If changing a term does not change who gets paid what in a downside case, the waterfall is decoration.
- **Takes.** 4 weeks to 12 months
- **Television.** TV replaces this with license fee versus deficit: a traditional network licenses at roughly 60-80% of cost and the studio deficits the rest against ownership, while streamers buy cost-plus (commonly quoted at 10-30% over budget, higher for big suppliers) and own everything.

### Sales estimates and pre-sales

The sales agent produces low/ask/take estimates per territory against the package, then sells territories for minimum guarantees, which a bank discounts to cash.

- **Who.** International Sales Agent; producer; buyers at markets (AFM, EFM Berlin, Marché du Film Cannes, TIFF)
- **Decides.** Which sales agent, what to give as commission and expense cap, whether to pre-sell key territories or hold them, and whether to attach a star specifically to raise a territory's number.
- **Engine.** Territory-by-territory estimate tables keyed to genre, budget, cast and director, with buyer creditworthiness driving how much a bank will lend against a contract. Swapping one cast member must recompute the estimates, and thus the financing.
- **Takes.** Estimates 1-2 weeks; markets 3 times a year
- **Television.** TV pre-sales are per-territory broadcaster licences or a single global streamer deal that forecloses all other sales; choosing global means no territory income ever.

### Tax incentive selection and application

Selecting a filming jurisdiction for its incentive and applying: Georgia 30% transferable credit; UK Audio-Visual Expenditure Credit at 34% (with the enhanced independent film credit at 53% for qualifying lower-budget films); Iceland 25%, or 35% where Icelandic spend is at least ISK 350 million with at least 30 working days and 10 shooting days in Iceland (scheme valid to 31 December 2028).

- **Who.** Producer; production accountant; incentive consultant; the jurisdiction's film office
- **Decides.** Where to shoot, whether to restructure spend or cast nationality to qualify, whether to monetise the credit by sale or loan, and how the choice affects the schedule and crew.
- **Engine.** Jurisdiction table with rate, qualifying spend rules, caps, per-project application deadlines, audit requirements, and payout timing (months after delivery) — plus the discount rate to cash it early. The incentive must alter where the film physically shoots, not just add money.
- **Takes.** Application 2-8 weeks; payout 3-18 months post-delivery
- **Television.** Series apply per season and must maintain qualification across years; a rate change between seasons can force a show to move country.

### Gap, bridge, interparty and completion bond

A bank cash-flows presales and tax credits; gap finance covers unsold territories against estimates; a completion guarantor bonds delivery for a fee (customarily around 2-3% of budget with a rebate — estimate) and takes takeover rights.

- **Who.** Bank/lender; completion guarantor; producer's attorney; sales agent
- **Decides.** How much gap to take and at what cost, which bonder, and whether to accept the bonder's cuts to schedule, cast or budget as a condition of bonding.
- **Engine.** Loan objects with LTV limits against estimates, interest, fees and a closing checklist; the bonder is an agent that reviews script, schedule and budget and can refuse to bond, demand contingency, or later take over the picture. Refusal must be a real failure branch.
- **Takes.** 6 weeks to 6 months
- **Television.** Studio-financed series need none of this; independent international TV drama uses the same toolkit as film.

### Co-production structuring

Structuring under a bilateral treaty or the European Convention to access both countries' funds and incentives, satisfying points tests on nationality of key creatives and spend split.

- **Who.** Producer; co-producer in the partner country; national film agency; treaty administrator
- **Decides.** Partner country and partner company, which department heads to give to the partner, spend split, ownership of copyright and territory splits.
- **Engine.** A points-test calculator per treaty, coupled to the actual crew and cast list and spend plan; failing the test by one point must cost real money. Co-production changes who is hireable — a real constraint on later casting and crewing.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months
- **Television.** TV co-productions frequently split by broadcaster territory, with each broadcaster demanding a version and editorial say — two sets of notes, permanently.

### Marketing and distribution pre-greenlight read

Marketing is asked before the greenlight: can this be sold in thirty seconds, what is the one-sheet, who is the audience, what is the rating, what date could it hold, and what would P&A cost.

- **Who.** Head of Marketing; Head of Distribution; international distribution; research department
- **Decides.** Whether to accept marketing's demanded changes (title, rating, ending, cast), and which release date to target.
- **Engine.** A marketing model that outputs an audience-segment interest profile and a P&A requirement from concept, cast and rating — feeding directly into the ultimates. Marketing's veto must be able to kill a script the creative side loves.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** The streamer equivalent is the programming/insights team forecasting subscriber acquisition and retention, and a slot on the release calendar against internal competition.

### Greenlight model and ultimates

A ten-year revenue projection ("ultimates") by window — theatrical domestic/international, PVOD, home ent, pay TV, free TV, streaming licence — against negative cost, P&A, participations, residuals, overhead and interest, producing break-even and expected return.

- **Who.** Studio Finance / FP&A; Head of Production; Business Affairs
- **Decides.** The budget number the studio will accept, what has to come out of the script to hit it, what the incentive must deliver, and whether a star's quote is affordable given their marginal effect on the projection.
- **Engine.** A full financial model with window-by-window revenue functions of budget, cast, genre, rating and season; studio accounting rules (customary distribution fees around 30-35%, overhead around 15% of negative cost, interest — treat as customary estimates) and a participation waterfall. The player must be able to move one input and watch break-even move.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks per pass; several passes
- **Television.** TV's model is licence-fee-versus-cost per episode plus library value across a series life, or for a streamer, forecast hours viewed and retention per dollar; there is no box office term at all.

### The greenlight decision (film)

A meeting (often after two to four prior vettings) at which the script, budget, schedule, cast, director, incentive and ultimates are approved or refused. The chairman/CEO usually has the last word; some studios require corporate parent approval above a threshold.

- **Who.** Greenlight committee: studio chairman/CEO, president of production, heads of marketing, distribution, finance, business affairs and legal
- **Decides.** Yes, no, or yes-at-a-lower-number; conditions (recast, cut ten days, get the rating down, bring the budget under a ceiling); and the pay-or-play trigger that makes the spending irreversible.
- **Engine.** A committee of executives with individual utility functions, budget authority thresholds, current slate pressure and political capital. The same project must be able to pass in one quarter and fail in another because the slate changed. Conditional greenlights must produce real re-work loops.
- **Takes.** The meeting is 60-90 minutes; the cycle 3-12 months
- **Television.** No single greenlight: TV climbs a ladder of commitments, each a separate decision by a separate group.

### Series pickup and licence-fee negotiation (TV)

After pilot or on a straight-to-series pitch, the buyer orders episodes and sets the licence fee, episode count, delivery dates, seasons optioned and backend. Broadcast decisions cluster around the May upfronts, though in 2026 CBS moved its schedule reveal to April 15 and NBC ran a traditional eight-pilot January-May cycle with screenings in late April.

- **Who.** Head of Programming/Content Chief; Head of Business Affairs at the network/streamer; studio's TV Business Affairs
- **Decides.** Episode count (6, 8, 10, 13, 22), licence fee versus ownership, straight-to-series versus pilot, launch window, and whether to accept a cost-plus deal that removes backend.
- **Engine.** An ordering ladder with real money at each rung: script order, script plus penalty, put pilot (penalty commonly $250,000-$500,000 and sometimes seven figures), pilot production commitment, backend penalty, series commitment, straight-to-series. Each rung buys a different probability of the next.
- **Takes.** Decision windows of days at upfronts; negotiation 2-10 weeks
- **Television.** This is the television-specific replacement for the film greenlight and behaves nothing like it.

### Pilot testing and scheduling

Finished pilots are tested with recruited audiences (dial testing, likeability, intent to view) and screened internally, then placed on a schedule against competitors and lead-ins.

- **Who.** Network Research department; Head of Scheduling; programming executives
- **Decides.** Whether to re-shoot or recast on the strength of test results, which night and time slot, and which existing show to lead out of.
- **Engine.** A test-score generator correlated imperfectly with actual performance — the key point is that testing is noisy, and the player must decide how much to trust it. Schedule position must change measured ratings by a modelled lead-in and competition effect.
- **Takes.** Testing 1-3 weeks; scheduling decided in the week before upfronts
- **Television.** Almost entirely broadcast/cable; streamers substitute internal taste plus algorithmic audience-size forecasts and have no time slot.

### Turnaround, abandonment and write-off

A studio drops a project and puts it in turnaround: another buyer may take it by repaying the accumulated development cost plus interest, usually within a short window, with changed-element payments if the package changes.

- **Who.** Studio Business Affairs; producer; the new buyer
- **Decides.** Whether to fight for turnaround rather than let it sit, who to take it to, whether to buy it back personally, and whether to accept the studio keeping a passive interest.
- **Engine.** A turnaround price object: cumulative development spend, interest accrual, a purchase window, and encumbrances (existing pay-or-play deals, attached elements). Old projects must sit dormant with a running cost and a real, priceable route out.
- **Takes.** Window commonly 30 days to 18 months; the drift before it can be years
- **Television.** TV projects usually just die at season's end; where turnaround exists the studio often retains distribution or a passive backend on the reincarnated show.

### Development slate management and burn

Running a slate: how many projects in active development, how much is committed to options, writers and materials, how many executives are on staff, and what the overhead costs per month.

- **Who.** Head of Development; President of Production; CFO of the production company (the mother's job)
- **Decides.** Slate size and genre mix, spend per project, when to kill something, whether to take a studio first-look that pays overhead, and how many creative executives to employ.
- **Engine.** A portfolio simulation: each project has a cost curve, a survival probability at each gate and a payoff distribution. Overhead burns monthly regardless. The engine must let a slate be structurally wrong — all one genre, all too expensive, all dependent on one buyer — and punish it.
- **Takes.** Continuous; reviewed quarterly
- **Television.** A TV slate must be timed to buyer development seasons and staffing season; a feature slate is timed to markets and to release-date availability.

### Relationships, general meetings and access

The unpaid infrastructure: generals, drinks, festivals, the water-bottle tour after a sale, and the favour ledger that decides whose call is returned.

- **Who.** Agent; manager; Creative Executive; the writer, actor or producer
- **Decides.** How to spend the week: writing, meeting, or being seen. Who to introduce to whom. Whether to burn a relationship by passing badly or going around someone.
- **Engine.** A relationship graph with strength, decay, debt and memory. Access must gate everything above: whose script gets read tonight, who takes the pitch, whose call about a lapse gets a yes. It must decay if unattended and be measurably rebuildable.
- **Takes.** 30-90 minutes per meeting; continuous
- **Television.** TV relationships concentrate on a small number of buyers who each hold the fate of many projects; film relationships spread across financiers, agents and festivals.

### Option spaces

**Origin of material** — 24 options

`Original idea by the player-writer` · `Original idea by a staff/company writer` · `Spec screenplay bought on the open market` · `Novel (published)` · `Novel (pre-publication manuscript or galley)` · `Non-fiction book` · `Magazine or newspaper article` · `Podcast` · `Comic book / graphic novel` · `Video game` · `Toy, brand or IP library title` · `Stage play or musical` · `Short film` · `Foreign-language film remake` · `Foreign TV format licence` · `Life rights of a living person` · `Public-domain work` · `Sequel/prequel to an owned title` · `Spin-off from an owned series` · `Documentary subject` · `Song or album` · `Social media/viral phenomenon` · `Studio-owned dormant library title` · `Court record or public-record story (no rights needed)`

**Rights vehicle** — 14 options

`No agreement (informal attachment)` · `Shopping agreement` · `Option agreement` · `Option-purchase agreement` · `Outright purchase / assignment` · `Work-for-hire commission` · `Life rights agreement with release` · `Depiction release only` · `Format licence` · `Remake/adaptation licence, limited territory` · `Quitclaim from a prior owner` · `If/come agreement (TV)` · `Co-development agreement with a partner producer` · `Public-domain declaration with counsel opinion`

**Written development materials** — 19 options

`Logline` · `One-pager / sell sheet` · `Look book / mood deck` · `Sizzle reel or proof-of-concept short` · `Treatment (5-30 pp)` · `Extended treatment / scriptment` · `Beat sheet` · `Card board (index cards)` · `Step outline` · `Series format (WGA payable)` · `Series bible (WGA payable)` · `Pilot script` · `Backup script` · `Feature first draft` · `Writer's take document` · `Pitch deck` · `Comps and audience memo` · `Budget top sheet` · `Financing plan one-pager`

**Feature buyer type** — 10 options

`Major studio` · `Studio specialty label` · `Mini-major` · `Streamer originals (film)` · `Independent financier/equity` · `International sales company with MG` · `Public film fund` · `High-net-worth private equity` · `Brand/advertiser-funded` · `Producer self-financing`

**Television buyer type** — 10 options

`Broadcast network (ABC, CBS, NBC, FOX, The CW)` · `Basic cable` · `Premium pay TV` · `Tier 1 SVOD (high-budget streaming)` · `Tier 2 SVOD (lower cost thresholds)` · `AVOD/FAST` · `International public broadcaster (BBC, RTÉ, RÚV, ABC AU)` · `International commercial broadcaster` · `Co-production between two territories` · `Syndication/first-run`

**TV commitment ladder (lowest to highest)** — 12 options

`General meeting interest` · `Development deal (paid script)` · `If/come agreement` · `Script order` · `Script order with penalty` · `Put pilot (penalty commonly $250k-$500k)` · `Pilot production commitment` · `Pilot plus backend series penalty` · `Straight-to-series order (part season)` · `Straight-to-series order (full season)` · `Two-season order` · `Limited series order`

**Writer deal structures** — 13 options

`Spec purchase` · `One-step deal (constrained: a first draft at 225% or less of minimum compels a paid rewrite)` · `Two-step deal (draft + rewrite)` · `Full step deal (story, first draft, rewrite, polish)` · `Weekly/week-to-week employment` · `Term employment (14/14, 20/26, 40/52)` · `Blind script commitment` · `If/come pilot agreement` · `First-look deal` · `Overall deal` · `Housekeeping/pod deal` · `Production-executive employment under Article 14` · `Development room seat (10-19 weeks guaranteed)`

**Coverage verdict and grid** — 9 options

`Recommend (script)` · `Consider (script)` · `Pass (script)` · `Recommend (writer)` · `Consider (writer)` · `Pass (writer)` · `Grid: premise / structure / characterization / dialogue / setting, each Excellent, Good, Fair or Poor` · `Consider with attachment` · `Recommend for a different department (e.g. TV rather than film)`

**Note types** — 15 options

`Structural note` · `Character note` · `Tone note` · `Dialogue note` · `Page-count note` · `Budget-driven note (cut the set piece)` · `Casting-driven note (make the lead 20 years older)` · `Rating note` · `Marketing note (we can't sell this ending)` · `Legal/clearance note` · `Standards & practices note (broadcast)` · `Comp-driven note (make it more like X)` · `Franchise note (make it more repeatable)` · `Notes on notes (filtered from a superior)` · `Contradictory note pair`

**Attachment strength** — 10 options

`Verbal interest` · `Read and liked, no commitment` · `Soft attachment (name may be used with financiers)` · `Letter of intent (subject to financing and dates)` · `Negotiated deal, not signed` · `Signed deal, contingent on financing close` · `Pay-or-play (fee owed regardless)` · `First position (this project wins date conflicts)` · `Second position` · `Signed and started (services commenced)`

**Financing instruments** — 17 options

`Studio balance sheet` · `Producer equity` · `Third-party equity (single or fund)` · `Slate financing` · `Pre-sale minimum guarantees` · `Gap loan against unsold estimates` · `Bridge loan against a signed contract` · `Tax-credit loan` · `Soft money grant` · `Development loan (repayable at principal photography)` · `Deferrals (cast, crew, vendors)` · `Co-production partner contribution` · `Product placement / brand funding` · `Crowdfunding` · `Completion-bonded senior debt` · `Mezzanine debt` · `Advance from a distributor`

**Development jurisdiction / territory** — 8 options

`United States (WGA MBA, studio buyout norms)` · `United Kingdom (Pact terms of trade; indie retains IP; broadcaster tariff 20-100% of budget)` · `Ireland (Screen Ireland development loans up to €75k, €125k with match)` · `Iceland (Icelandic Film Centre grants; 25% or 35% production reimbursement)` · `Canada (Telefilm, CMF, provincial credits)` · `Australia (Screen Australia $10k-$50k narrative development)` · `Continental Europe (Creative Europe MEDIA, Eurimages, national funds)` · `Co-production across two of the above`

**Greenlight decision-makers** — 12 options

`Studio chairman/CEO` · `President of the Motion Picture Group` · `Greenlight committee (production, marketing, distribution, finance, legal, business affairs)` · `Corporate parent CEO above a budget threshold` · `Streamer content chief` · `Streamer film/series head plus data/insights` · `Network entertainment president plus scheduling` · `Head of a specialty label` · `Independent financier's investment committee` · `Sales company's board plus the bank's credit committee` · `Public fund's assessment panel` · `The producer alone (self-financed)`

**Turnaround outcomes** — 9 options

`Renew development and continue` · `Change writer and continue` · `Shelve indefinitely (dormant, cost accruing)` · `Put into turnaround at cost plus interest` · `Sell to another buyer with changed-element payments` · `Producer buys it back personally` · `Write off and abandon` · `Let the underlying option lapse` · `Reversion to the writer under separated rights`

**Pitch venue and format** — 12 options

`In-person studio office pitch` · `Video call pitch` · `Pitch with a deck` · `Pitch with a sizzle` · `Pitch on the phone (short-form)` · `Pitch over a meal` · `Pitch at a festival or market` · `Pitch by a producer without the writer present` · `Writer pitches alone` · `Pitch to a junior executive first (screening meeting)` · `Pitch to the decision-maker directly` · `Pitch in a general meeting (unplanned)`

**Draft labels** — 13 options

`Story/treatment` · `First draft` · `Second draft` · `Revised draft (date-stamped)` · `Rewrite` · `Page-one rewrite` · `Polish` · `Producer's draft` · `Studio draft` · `Network/streamer draft` · `Table draft (TV)` · `Production draft` · `Locked pages with coloured revisions (white, blue, pink, yellow, green, goldenrod, buff, salmon, cherry, double white...)`

**Credits available to a writer** — 11 options

`Written by` · `Screenplay by` · `Story by` · `Screen Story by` · `Adaptation by` · `Based on characters created by` · `Created by (TV)` · `Developed by (TV)` · `Teleplay by` · `Story Editor / Executive Story Editor (title, not credit)` · `No credit`

**Producer credits given during development** — 11 options

`Producer` · `Executive Producer` · `Co-Executive Producer (TV)` · `Co-Producer` · `Associate Producer` · `Consulting Producer` · `Producer credit to a financier` · `Producer credit to a star or their company` · `Producer credit to a manager` · `Executive Producer to the rights holder` · `Created by/Developed by attached to the format`

**What can be cut to hit a budget number** — 13 options

`Cut shooting days` · `Cut a set piece` · `Cut a location and combine scenes` · `Change territory for the incentive` · `Reduce cast size` · `Lower the rating (broader audience, cheaper marketing)` · `Recast a lower-quote lead` · `Reduce VFX shot count` · `Move to stage from location` · `Shorten the script (fewer pages)` · `Take deferrals from above-the-line` · `Reduce episode count (TV)` · `Reduce the room size (TV, floor of 3 writers/10 weeks applies)`

**Reasons a project dies in development** — 17 options

`Buyer's regime change` · `Similar project set up elsewhere` · `Attachment walked` · `Budget wouldn't come down` · `Option lapsed` · `Rights defect in the chain of title` · `Notes rounds exhausted the money` · `Comparable film flopped` · `Star's quote unaffordable` · `Incentive rules changed` · `Financing gap never closed` · `Bonder refused to bond` · `Marketing couldn't sell it` · `Writer refused a note` · `Public scandal attached to a key element` · `Slate is full for the release window` · `Cost-plus terms made the backend worthless`

### What gets got wrong

- Development is not one funnel. Film runs idea → rights → script → package → financing plan → a single greenlight meeting. Television runs idea → attachments → pitch → if/come or script order → pilot script → pilot or straight-to-series → room → pickup, with money and a decision-maker at each rung. Building TV as film-with-episodes is the single largest design error available here.
- The $1 option is illegal for professional writers. The WGA MBA requires at least 10% of the applicable theatrical minimum for a feature option up to 18 months (10% more per renewal), and in television 5% of minimum for the first 180 days, 10% for each further 180 days. Sub-scale options only exist for non-professional writers and non-signatory producers.
- Agency packaging fees are dead. Agents lost the right to negotiate them on WGA-covered projects as of June 30 2022, are capped at 10% commission, and may own no more than 20% of an affiliated production company. Any packaging economy in the game must be modelled as attachment-building and commission, not as a 3/3/10 fee.
- One-step deals are constrained by contract, not custom. Under the 2026 MBA, a first-draft hire (or purchase from a professional writer) at 225% or less of the applicable minimum obliges the company to employ that writer for a rewrite at no less than the rewrite minimum. A cheap one-and-done screenplay deal is not available at scale prices.
- The if/come rule changes on January 1 2027: the writer cannot be made exclusive and the company is in no position until it has paid the first pilot-script step or bought the script. If the game's calendar crosses that date, the rules of the board must change mid-game.
- A development room has a legal floor: three writer-producers including the showrunner, each guaranteed at least ten consecutive weeks, at premium rates that cannot be credited against later episodic fees. Twenty weeks or more and it is a regular room. "Mini-room" is a cost with a minimum, not a free option.
- Credit is not the employer's to give. The company proposes; the WGA arbitrates anonymously from the stored drafts, and arbitration is automatic when a production executive claims credit. The father being the greatest screenwriter alive does not let him award himself "Written by" over a subsequent writer, and this must be a system reading real draft diffs, not a flag.
- Separated rights are an annuity, not a footnote. In television the "Created by" writer receives series sequel payments per episode for the life of the show ($5,109 per 60-minute episode for 5/2/26-5/1/27). Model it and a creator's fortune makes sense; skip it and the economics of the whole career are wrong.
- Payment for a delivered draft cannot be made contingent on approval or on obtaining financing, must arrive within seven days, and accrues 1.5% monthly interest if late. "We'll pay you when we close the money" is a Guild claim, not a strategy.
- An LOI binds almost nothing. Financiers know this, so an LOI, a soft attachment and a pay-or-play deal must have three different values in the financing model and three different collapse probabilities. If all three read as "attached", the packaging system is decorative.
- Pay-or-play is irreversible spend. Once a director or star is pay-or-play the fee is owed whether the film shoots or not — the moment the studio stops being able to walk away cheaply. It should be a visible, dated, one-way door.
- Turnaround has a price: accumulated development cost plus interest, inside a window, with changed-element payments if the package moves. Dormant projects must carry a running number, not a vague "in development" tag.
- UK commissions are not US buyouts. Under the Pact terms of trade the independent producer retains the IP and the broadcaster pays a tariff of roughly 20-100% of budget; a US-style total buyout misprices every UK project and destroys the library value that makes a UK indie worth buying.
- Streaming cost-plus removes backend. A global cost-plus deal (commonly quoted at 10-30% over budget, higher for large suppliers) makes a smash hit and a quiet failure worth nearly the same to the producer, except in reputation and renewal odds. If the game pays streamer success bonuses like syndication, it is simulating the wrong decade.
- A broadcast hour is 42-44 minutes of content in five or six acts with act-outs; a streaming hour is 45-70 minutes with none. Same "one hour" label, different scripts, different page counts, different WGA fee tiers, different structure notes. The engine must know which it is writing.
- WGA fee tiers turn on hard thresholds: theatrical high budget is costs at or above $5,000,000; streaming Tier 1 versus Tier 2 turns on runtime bands and cost floors ($2.1M for 20-35 minute programs, $3.8M for 36-65 minutes, and so on). A budget cut of one dollar across a threshold changes what the writers must be paid.
- Pilots and backup scripts are priced off the same table with multipliers: a pilot script is 150% of the applicable story and/or teleplay minimum, a backup script 115%, and on pilots 10% of the first installment is advanced at commencement. These are multipliers on live numbers, not separate constants.
- Coverage is a permanent record. A pass written by a tired reader at one studio can be searched by the next studio's story department. Model reader identity, fatigue, taste and turnaround, or the read is a dice roll with a nice name.
- The greenlight is a committee with a hierarchy, not a threshold check. Marketing and distribution can veto a script the creative team loves; the chairman can override the committee; corporate approval may be required above a budget line. The same project must be able to pass in one quarter and fail in the next because the slate, not the script, changed.
- Broadcast development still runs on a calendar. In the 2026 cycle NBC ran a traditional eight-pilot January-May season with company screenings in the last week of April, while CBS moved its schedule reveal to April 15 and works on long-lead development. Missing a window means waiting a year — the clock must be a real constraint.
- Development money is spent whether or not anything is made. Options, writing fees, bibles, sizzles, legal and overhead burn monthly; a slate has to be modelled as a portfolio with survival probabilities at each gate, or the mother's company will look profitable while doing nothing.
- Life rights are not copyright. They buy cooperation, likeness and a release, not the story; a competing unauthorized version is legally possible, and refusing to buy them raises litigation and E&O exposure rather than making the film impossible.
- Public development money has strings and a clock: nationality tests, assessment rounds with fixed deadlines, and repayment on the first day of principal photography, often at a premium. Taking Screen Ireland or Icelandic Film Centre money constrains where the film can later be made and who can make it.
- The tax incentive should move the production, not just the balance sheet. Iceland pays 25%, or 35% where Icelandic spend is at least ISK 350 million with at least 30 working days and 10 shooting days in country; Georgia is 30% transferable; the UK AVEC is 34%, with 53% for qualifying independent films. Rates without qualification rules and payout lag are just a discount coupon.
- Figures cited here are the WGA 2026 Theatrical and Television Basic Agreement first-period minimums (5/2/26-5/1/27) unless stated; completion bond fees (~2-3% of budget), studio distribution fees (~30-35%), studio overhead (~15% of negative cost) and development-loan repayment premiums are customary market figures and should be treated as estimates and made configurable, not hard-coded truths.

---

## 2. The screenplay itself

*format, structure, the room, drafts, revisions, and the production script*

### Logline lock

Compress premise to one or two sentences: protagonist, want, opposition, irony, arena. Sits atop every later document and is the sentence buyers repeat back.
