### What gets got wrong

- Role size is not a label, it is a measurement. Scene count, line count and eighths of a page determine it, and those numbers must be derived from the script object. If the player can type 'guest star' into a field without the script supporting it, the whole ladder is decoration.
- Character and performer must be separate objects with a history of bindings between them. Almost every interesting process in this domain — recasting, doubling, digital replicas, a character outliving the actor, an actor playing twins — is impossible if a character IS an actor.
- Under-five has a hard, checkable rule: five lines or fewer AND fewer than fifty words. Exceeding it reclassifies the role to a full part at a large pay jump. The engine can check this against the actual dialogue, so it should — a writer adding one word must be able to cost the production real money.
- A day performer who works a third day must be reclassified. Scheduling is therefore a contract-tier decision, not just a calendar decision, and moving one scene can reclassify a performer.
- The SAG-AFTRA minimum inverts with the guarantee: half-hour series contracts pay $4,456/week guaranteed 13 or more but $5,947/week guaranteed 6 (07/01/26-06/30/27). Modelling it the intuitive way round — more episodes, more per week — is wrong and reverses every casting economy.
- Schedule F is an election, not a tier: above the break the deal is exempt from many working-condition minimums. The 2026 MOA set the break at $80,000 theatrical, $37,500 half-hour TV and new media, $45,000 one hour and longer. Above it, the overtime engine switches off for that performer.
- 'Major role performer' is a TV-only category with an oddly specific scope — half-hour or one-hour scripted dramatic series on ABC, CBS, FBC, NBC in prime time or The CW, prime-time pay TV in its second or later season, 20-35 or 36-65 minute high-budget SVOD, and the first season of prime-time pay TV. It is priced at 10% above the day rate for a guaranteed 5 days (half-hour) or 8 days (one hour): $7,057 and $11,290 for 07/01/26-06/30/27.
- Television is not film with more episodes, and this domain is where that is most true. Film has a cast; television has an architecture that determines the weekly payroll for up to seven years. The ensemble-architecture choice is upstream of the budget, not downstream of the story.
- Billing position is a scarce resource with collisions. Two performers cannot both have last position. If the engine does not detect the collision and force money to be traded for placement, billing is prose.
- Favoured nations propagates. Improving one member's term must automatically improve every other member's, and the player must see the propagated cost before agreeing. A favoured-nations grant the engine does not propagate is the single most dishonest thing this domain could contain.
- Options are dated and expire whether or not the player is paying attention. A missed exercise date releases the performer into the market where a rival can sign them. If the clock does not enforce it without prompting, options are not real.
- California Labor Code 2855 caps specific enforcement of a personal services contract at seven years from commencement of service. That is why series options run to six or seven seasons and no further, and why the season-three renegotiation is the standard leverage point.
- The test option deal is negotiated in full BEFORE the actor is allowed to read for the network. Several performers hold complete, binding deals for the same role at once and only one converts. Model it as contract-attached-to-probability or the whole pilot season is meaningless.
- Recurring performers increasingly do the work series regulars used to do, at day-player money. This is a live industry pressure, not a historical footnote, and a simulation that only models the clean ladder (co-star → guest star → recurring → regular) will miss the reason agents fight over it.
- A quote is a ratchet the agent protects. Taking a job below quote must actually lower what the next production offers, or 'quote protection' is a word with nothing reading it — the exact failure this project exists to prevent.
- Arc shape is a state machine, not an adjective. A positive arc must pass through belief-in-the-Lie, first doubt, cost, relapse and truth. If the engine will mark an arc complete with states skipped, then 'redemption arc' is a dropdown entry and nothing more.
- Want and Need must be different objects with different types. The Want is a goal in the world the character pursues; the Need is a predicate that, once true, changes which choice the character makes. If both are strings in a dossier, neither does anything.
- Flipping the Lie must invert the third-act choice. That is the falsifiability test for this entire domain: change the character's false belief and the climax must come out differently, or the dossier is decoration.
- The relationship graph is directed and asymmetric. What A wants from B is rarely what B wants from A, and modelling it as an undirected edge with one 'closeness' number destroys most of the drama available.
- Unused edges in the relationship graph are inventory. A long-running series banks the pairing that has never shared a scene and spends it in season five. A simulation that does not track never-met pairs cannot model why that episode is an event.
- Nudity, simulated sex and digital replica consents are gates, not fields. No rider, no scene — the scene must be rewritten, doubled or cut, with a cost. Consent withdrawn on the day must be able to cost a shoot day.
- Digital replica consent must be scoped to named uses and a duration, and the post pipeline must check it before a replica shot can exist. Employment-based and independently created replicas have different consent regimes, and a synthetic performer used in place of hiring carries a justification obligation.
- Minors' hour limits constrain the schedule, and a studio teacher is a required crew position on school days. Coogan takes a statutory 15% of gross in California into trust before the performer is paid. Casting an of-age performer to look younger removes all of it, which is why the industry does it.
- Stand-in and photo double are $270/day, general background $231, special ability $241 (07/01/26-06/30/27) — but the bumps are where the real variance lives: wet, smoke, body makeup, wardrobe changes, props, personal vehicle, hazardous requests. Background pay is a formula, not a rate.
- A face-visible frame containing a photo double reclassifies the day and repays it. The rule is enforceable at the shot level, so the shot list must carry who must actually be present.
- Script clearance is a per-name risk record and a change cost that propagates. A refused name kept anyway must raise the E&O premium and the litigation hazard. If clearance is a checkbox, it is not a process.
- The table read is where a character is discovered not to work, and the consequences are real: a cut, a rewrite, occasionally a recast. A table read that only produces prose is a wasted event.
- Character continuity failures must produce unusable takes at edit time, not notes. The script supervisor's log is only real if the editor can hit a mismatch.
- Dialect proficiency decays without coaching and must be checked per take, producing failed takes and ADR. ADR match quality should fall with time since the shoot. That chain — coach hours → proficiency → takes → post cost — is what makes 'accent work' a system.
- Audience character scores must be computed from the character's real properties (screen time, arc completion, whether the Need was articulated, the performer's standing), not drawn. Otherwise reshooting for a character is a slot machine.
- Performer standing and character attachment are separate measures that can diverge. A beloved character on a disliked performer changes the recasting and write-out arithmetic, and collapsing them into one number throws away the most interesting case.
- A holdout must halt production and cost money per day, or a season-three renegotiation is theatre. Leverage should be computed from ratings, attachment, cost of write-out and episodes already committed.
- The Day Out of Days grid is the hinge between the story and the money. Moving one scene changes someone's day count, which changes their fee and possibly their contract tier. If the DOOD is not live, the schedule and the payroll are two unconnected fictions.
- Archetype sets are not interchangeable and should not be merged into one list. Propp permits one person in several spheres; Dramatica forbids two Protagonists; Vogler's Shadow is a function, Jung's Shadow is a psychic content. Flattening them produces a list nothing can validate against.
- The mother (production company), the father (lot and production side, and the screenwriter) and the player-as-actor see this domain through three different interfaces onto the same objects: the father authors the dossier and the arc, the mother approves the tier, the guarantee, the billing and the favoured-nations exposure, and the actor holds the quote, the riders, the technique and the analysis. The same character record must be legible and editable at three different levels of authority, with actions that are illegal for one and routine for another.

---

## 5. Script breakdown and scheduling

### Script lock and scene numbering

Freeze scene numbers and page positions so every downstream document points at the same thing. New scenes take A-suffixes (A12 sits between 12 and 13). Cut scenes become OMITTED and keep their number forever.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator (TV) or Production Secretary with the writer; 1st AD signs off; Producer authorises
- **Decides.** Whether to lock at all (many indies never do), at which draft, whether to renumber on a page-one rewrite, and whether OMITTED scenes stay visible on the board as zero-eighth rows.
- **Engine.** Scene ids immutable post-lock, with a lock version stamp. A suffix generator that inserts A/AA between neighbours without renumbering. Omitted flag that keeps the row and zeroes its eighths. Every strip, sheet and DOOD cell stores the scene id, never its ordinal.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours to lock a feature; renumbering a locked script is half a day and is avoided
- **Television.** TV locks per episode and per block. Numbers restart each episode, so ids must be episode-qualified (104-A12) or cross-boarding collides two scene 12s on one day.

### Page-eighths measurement

Measures each scene in eighths of a page. Any scene, however short, counts at least 1/8. Eighths total per day, per set, per character, and per unit.

- **Who.** 1st AD, checked against the scheduling software's auto-count
- **Decides.** Trust the software count or override by judgement; whether to re-measure after a revision; the eighths-per-day target the whole board is then sized against.
- **Engine.** A real text-layout model (12pt Courier, fixed lines per page) so eighths derive from the rendered page rather than a guess. Round per scene, not per day. Keep eighths and estimated hours as separate fields — conflating them is the classic modelling error.
- **Takes.** Seconds in software; 2-4 hours by hand for a 100-page feature
- **Television.** Hour drama runs roughly 55-66 pages an episode, half-hour single-cam around 30, multi-cam 45-50 because its format is double-spaced and plays far faster per page.

### Script marking (element tagging)

Reads the script line by line and tags every noun that costs money or time into a category — cast, background, stunts, vehicles, props, wardrobe, SFX, VFX, animals, special equipment, and the rest.

- **Who.** 1st AD on features; the block's 1st AD on TV; sometimes a dedicated scheduler or the 2nd AD
- **Decides.** Which category an ambiguous item lands in (a gun as prop, as armourer call, or as stunt), whether to tag mentioned-but-unseen items, and granularity: THREE POLICE CARS as one element or three.
- **Engine.** A tag is a (scene text-span, category, element id) triple, not a string in a notes field. An alias table so 'Jack's Zippo' and 'ZIPPO' resolve to one element. Each category carries its own required fields, lead time, rate card and approval gate.
- **Takes.** 8-16 hours for a 100-page feature; 4-8 hours for an hour episode
- **Television.** TV tags each episode against a persistent series element library; recurring sets, cast and props carry ids forward and only deltas get new ones.

### Breakdown sheet assembly

One sheet per scene: sheet number, scene number, INT/EXT, day/night, set, location, story day, page eighths, synopsis, and every tagged element grouped by category.

- **Who.** 1st AD; 2nd AD distributes; every department head reads their own rows
- **Decides.** Sheet granularity (one per scene versus one per set-and-time grouping), how much synopsis to write, and which categories are shown to which department.
- **Engine.** The sheet is a derived view over Scene x Element, never a separate authored record — editing a sheet must write back to the tag. Per-department filtered renders. A dirty flag that re-derives when a scene or element changes.
- **Takes.** Generated instantly once tagging is done; 1-2 days of departmental read-and-query follow
- **Television.** Sheets reissue every episode; department heads read only the diff against the last episode, so the engine needs a sheet-level changelog.

### Element catalogue and de-duplication

Collapses every tag in the script into a master list per category, merging spellings and near-duplicates, so the board can count elements rather than mentions.

- **Who.** 1st AD with the Production Coordinator; department heads confirm their own
- **Decides.** What is one element and what is two; whether HERO CAR and JACK'S CAR are the same vehicle; whether to split an element that appears in two conditions (clean suit, bloodied suit).
- **Engine.** A merge key with a manual override and an audit of every merge. Elements carry prep days, wrap days, day rate, quantity, and an availability calendar. Splitting an element must rewrite the tags that pointed at it, not orphan them.
- **Takes.** 4-8 hours, plus a week of corrections as departments read their lists
- **Television.** Series carry a standing catalogue across seasons; a merge decision made in season one still binds in season four, so the catalogue needs to be a save-level object, not a script-level one.

### Cast numbering

Assigns every speaking role a permanent cast number, roughly in order of importance: 1 is the lead. The number is what appears on strips, call sheets and the DOOD from then on.

- **Who.** 1st AD, with the Casting Director and Producer
- **Decides.** The ordering itself — which is political as well as practical — and whether stunt doubles, photo doubles and silent bits get numbers in the same series or a separate one.
- **Engine.** A stable cast-number registry per production, with a separate numbering space for doubles and background. Board strips reference cast numbers; the DOOD is keyed on them. Renumbering must cascade to every strip and sheet.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours; contested for a week
- **Television.** Series regulars hold their numbers across the whole run; guest cast get numbers per episode and are commonly renumbered each script, so the engine needs season-stable and episode-local pools.

### INT/EXT and day/night classification

Classifies each scene by location type and time of day from the slug line, which together drive strip colour, lighting package, crew call and how the day can be built.

- **Who.** 1st AD reads it off the slug line; DP and Production Designer challenge it
- **Decides.** Whether to shoot a written EXT NIGHT as day-for-night, a written EXT as a stage build, or an INT with a window as a de facto exterior that the sun controls.
- **Engine.** Classification is a scene field, but the shooting method is a separate field (as-written, day-for-night, night-for-day, stage-for-location, LED volume). Strip colour derives from the first; lighting cost, crew size and daylight constraints derive from the second.
- **Takes.** Minutes; the day-for-night argument takes a week
- **Television.** TV leans harder on stage-for-location and standing sets because eight-day episodes cannot afford weather risk; features gamble on real exteriors more often.

### Story day assignment

Numbers the script's own chronology — D1, N1, D2 — so that continuity of costume, injury, hair and time-of-day is tracked when the board shoots those scenes months apart.

- **Who.** Script Supervisor, with the 1st AD, Costume Designer and Hair/Makeup heads
- **Decides.** Where one story day ends and the next begins when the script is vague; whether a montage is one story day or several; how many story days the piece actually has.
- **Engine.** A story-day axis independent of both script order and shoot order. Costume, makeup and continuity state attach to (character, story day). Scheduling two scenes on the same shoot day with different story days must flag a change and cost the time it takes.
- **Takes.** 4-8 hours for a feature, revisited every revision
- **Television.** Serialised TV runs story days across episodes, so story day 14 may be shot in block 2 and block 4; the axis must span the season, not the script.

### Set-to-location assignment

Binds each script set to a real address, stage, backlot street or standing set. Several sets may share one location; one set may be split across two.

- **Who.** Location Manager with the Production Designer, 1st AD and Line Producer
- **Decides.** Stage or practical, which of several scouted options, whether to combine sets into one location to kill a company move, and whether to build rather than find.
- **Engine.** Set is a script object; Location is a world object with an address, travel time from base, parking, permit regime, cost per day, available windows, and a sun-obstruction profile. The board's grouping key is the location, not the set.
- **Takes.** Scouting 2-6 weeks; the assignment itself is settled in the tech scout
- **Television.** TV amortises standing sets hard: a show with six standing stage sets can absorb weather and rewrites that a location-heavy feature cannot.

### Strip creation

Turns each scene, or a group of scenes that shoot together, into one board strip carrying scene number, INT/EXT, D/N, set, one-line description, page eighths, cast numbers and location.

- **Who.** 1st AD
- **Decides.** Whether to combine scenes onto one strip (same set, same time, continuous action) or keep them separate for finer ordering control.
- **Engine.** Strip is a container over scene ids with derived eighths, derived cast union, derived colour and an author-set estimated duration. Combining and splitting strips must preserve the underlying scene ids so the DOOD and the script stay truthful.
- **Takes.** Generated instantly; 2-4 hours of manual combining
- **Television.** On cross-boarded blocks a single strip can hold scenes from two episodes; the strip must carry the episode alongside the scene.

### Strip colouring

Colours each strip from its INT/EXT and day/night pair, so the whole board reads at a glance: white INT day, yellow EXT day, blue INT night, green EXT night in the Movie Magic default.

- **Who.** Derived by the software; the 1st AD sets the palette
- **Decides.** Which palette convention the production uses — the generic-standard swap puts green on EXT night and blue on INT night — and what colour dawn, dusk and magic hour take.
- **Engine.** Colour is a pure function of (intExt, dayNight) through a palette lookup, never a stored per-strip choice. Banners, day breaks (black), holidays (red) and idle days (grey) are strip kinds, not colours. If the player can paint a strip by hand, the board is decorative.
- **Takes.** Instant
- **Television.** None; the palette is the same. Networks and studios sometimes add a per-episode tint over the colour, which must be a second visual channel, not a replacement.

### Shot estimate and per-scene timing

Estimates how long each scene actually takes: number of setups, whether it is a oner, whether a lighting relight sits between setups, how many company moves within the location.

- **Who.** 1st AD, from the director's shot list and the DP's lighting approach
- **Decides.** Setup count per scene, minutes per setup, whether to schedule for the director's stated shot list or for what that director historically shoots.
- **Engine.** Estimated hours = f(setups, setup class, cast count, stunt/SFX/VFX flags, child hours, actor's historic takes-per-setup, director's historic setups-per-day). Hold a per-director and per-DP pace model that learns from shot days. This number, not eighths, sizes the day.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days with the director; revised after every tech scout
- **Television.** TV directors are measured against a fixed episode length and a house pace; a director who shoots 22 setups a day on a show that needs 32 is not invited back.

### Day sizing

Sets how much work goes into one shooting day: target eighths, target setups, target elapsed hours from crew call to wrap, and the tolerance before the day is overloaded.

- **Who.** 1st AD with the Line Producer and UPM
- **Decides.** Pages-per-day target, the shooting-day length (10, 11, 12 hours), whether the production runs a five- or six-day week, and how much slack to leave for the unknown.
- **Engine.** A day-capacity model with hard hours, soft hours, and an overrun distribution. Overloaded days must actually run long and generate overtime postings, meal penalties and turnaround pressure, not just a red warning label.
- **Takes.** Settled in the first week of prep, argued over for the whole schedule
- **Television.** Network hour drama sizes to roughly 8 shoot days an episode at 5-6 pages a day; streaming hour drama runs 9-12; half-hour single-cam 4-5; multi-cam shoots the whole episode in one audience night. Features run 2-3 pages a day at studio scale, 5-8 on low budgets.

### Board ordering (the scheduling solve)

Orders the strips into shooting days to minimise cost and risk: group by location, then by set, then by day/night, then by cast, honouring every hard constraint and trading off the soft ones.

- **Who.** 1st AD, approved by UPM, Line Producer, Producer and director
- **Decides.** The whole shape of the shoot — what shoots when, in what order, at what pace, with which compromises accepted and which fought for.
- **Engine.** A constraint solver over strip permutations, not a sort. Hard constraints (availability, child hours, turnaround, permits, build completion) must be infeasible to violate; soft ones (cast holds, move count, night blocks) carry weights, and the objective is money plus a risk term. Show the player the cost delta of every alternative.
- **Takes.** 3-5 days for a first pass; 6-10 weeks of revision across prep
- **Television.** TV solves the same problem under a fixed calendar: the air date is immovable, so the solver optimises within a fixed day count rather than choosing the day count.

### Day break and banner insertion

Places the black day-break strips that cut the board into shooting days, and white banner strips carrying company moves, holidays, travel, unit splits and warnings.

- **Who.** 1st AD
- **Decides.** Where each day ends — moving a break by one strip moves the whole tail of the schedule — and what notes get a banner rather than a strip note.
- **Engine.** Day breaks are ordered board objects, so inserting or moving one re-derives dates, calls, DOOD codes, turnaround and costs downstream in one pass. Banners must be schedulable objects with duration (a move costs hours) rather than text decorations.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** TV boards carry episode-boundary banners as well, and a banner for the stage turnover between blocks.

### Day Out of Days generation

Derives a matrix of cast against shooting days, marking each cell Start, Work, Hold, Travel, Rehearsal, Drop, Pickup and Finish, with totals of work days, hold days and paid days per performer.

- **Who.** 2nd AD maintains it; 1st AD owns it; Production Accountant and agents read it
- **Decides.** Nothing directly — it is derived. What the player decides is the board that produces it, and whether the resulting shape of holds is affordable.
- **Engine.** DOOD is a pure derivation from the board plus each performer's contract type. It must recompute on every board change. Totals feed the payroll model directly: a hold day for a weekly performer posts a full day's salary to the ledger whether or not they are used.
- **Takes.** Instant once derived; reissued with every schedule revision
- **Television.** TV runs a DOOD per episode and a cumulative DOOD per block or season, because a regular's episodic guarantee is counted across episodes, not days.

### Cast availability and holds

Records each performer's available window, conflicting jobs, promised days off, and the days the production holds them exclusively. Holds cost money whether used or not.

- **Who.** 2nd AD and Production Coordinator against talent agents; Casting Director negotiates
- **Decides.** Whose availability to build the board around, whose to work around, when to release a performer, and whether to pay a hold rather than lose a day to their absence.
- **Engine.** Each performer holds an availability calendar with hard blackouts, soft preferences, an agreed number of work days, and a rate. The solver treats a lead's blackout as hard and a supporting player's as a weighted soft constraint with a recast escape.
- **Takes.** Ongoing across prep; a star's window can take months to pin
- **Television.** Series regulars are held for the production period and paid per episode regardless, so TV holds cost nothing marginal; guest cast holds behave like features.

### Drop and pickup

Stops paying a performer through a long gap by formally dropping them after one block of work and picking them up later, subject to a contractual minimum gap.

- **Who.** 2nd AD proposes; UPM and the performer's agent agree; Production Accountant executes
- **Decides.** Whether to drop at all, where the drop and pickup fall, and whether the saving beats the risk that the performer takes other work in the gap.
- **Engine.** A drop requires N intervening free days (widely cited as ten under SAG-AFTRA weekly terms — verify against the current Codified Basic Agreement) and must be legal before the DOOD writes WD/PW codes. If the gap is short, the engine must pay through rather than silently allow it.
- **Takes.** Decided when the board is near-final; renegotiated with every reshuffle
- **Television.** Rare in episodic TV; guarantee structures and episodic options replace it. It appears in limited series and long-form movies.

### Consecutive employment and weekly conversion

Applies the rule that once a performer starts, intervening days are paid whether worked or not, and that a day performer worked enough days is converted to weekly terms.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Accountant; 2nd AD supplies the day counts
- **Decides.** Whether to book a role as day, three-day (television), weekly or run-of-show, which changes cost, hold exposure and how freely the board can move that character.
- **Engine.** Contract type is a performer-level object that changes how the DOOD prices its own cells. Engagement rules must run as real predicates over the board — cross a threshold and the cost model changes shape, which the solver can then exploit or avoid.
- **Takes.** Decided at booking; recomputed on every board revision
- **Television.** Television has the three-day performer category that features do not, and series regular contracts price per episode rather than per day.

### Turnaround calculation

Enforces the minimum rest between wrap and the next call, per performer and per crew agreement. It is the rule that silently controls how fast a schedule can walk through the day.

- **Who.** 1st AD sets calls with it; 2nd AD applies it to cast; UPM authorises violations
- **Decides.** Whether the next day's call is legal, whether to move a call later and lose daylight, whether to swap a scene, or whether to pay a forced-call penalty.
- **Engine.** Per-person rest clocks, not a per-day flag: turnaround runs from individual wrap, so a late-wrapping actor blocks an early call for that actor only. Hold separate minima per agreement class, and a weekend-rest rule that spans days off. Violations post penalties to the ledger.
- **Takes.** Computed every night once wrap time is known
- **Television.** None in rule. TV's fixed calendar makes violations more likely at block boundaries, where a Friday night wrap collides with a Monday morning stage call.

### Forced call decision

Deliberately breaking turnaround and paying the penalty because the alternative — losing the location, the actor or the daylight — costs more.

- **Who.** UPM with the Line Producer; 1st AD raises it; the performer or department can refuse
- **Decides.** Whether to break rest at all, for whom, and how much penalty exposure to accept for how much recovered schedule.
- **Engine.** A priced decision: penalty amount per person per class against the modelled cost of the alternative. The penalty posts to the ledger. Repeat violations must damage crew fatigue, error rates and the production's standing with the guild, or the choice is free and therefore fake.
- **Takes.** Decided in the last hour of a shooting day
- **Television.** Same mechanic; showrunners bear it more often because the air date cannot move.

### Split-day construction

Builds a day whose call sits in the afternoon so the company shoots exteriors into dusk and interiors into the night, crossing the day/night line inside one call.

- **Who.** 1st AD, cleared with UPM and department heads
- **Decides.** The call time, which scenes sit either side of sunset, and whether the split is deep enough to reach the night work before overtime.
- **Engine.** A day whose scenes carry both day and night designators must resolve against a real sun-position model for that latitude and date. The engine needs civil twilight times, not a static 'night starts at 8'. Setups after the light goes need a different lighting package and crew.
- **Takes.** Built at the board stage; adjusted daily against sunset
- **Television.** TV uses splits to avoid full night weeks that would break the five-day rhythm and blow the episode's day count.

### Walking to nights and back

Shifts the call later across successive days — typically two to three hours a day — so the company reaches night calls without breaking turnaround, then walks back after the night block.

- **Who.** 1st AD, with UPM and the Line Producer
- **Decides.** How fast to walk, whether to burn a day off instead of walking, and whether to pay turnaround penalties to jump straight to nights.
- **Engine.** Walking is a turnaround-constrained arithmetic sequence over calls. The engine must also model the human cost: fatigue accumulating differently on a walk than on a jump, and performance and error rates degrading with circadian disruption.
- **Takes.** 3-5 days each way
- **Television.** TV usually clusters nights at the end of a week so the weekend absorbs the walk back; features can afford a mid-week reset.

### Night shoot planning

Plans a full night block: the lighting package, generators, condors, neighbourhood notification, noise curfews, and the hard wrap before dawn light contaminates the shot.

- **Who.** 1st AD, Gaffer, Key Grip, Location Manager and the Location Department's community relations
- **Decides.** How many consecutive nights, whether to build lighting the day before, whether to shoot day-for-night instead, and the hard out time.
- **Engine.** Night days must cost more: larger electric package, condor and generator rentals, night premiums in some agreements, catering, and a curfew that truncates the day. Dawn is a hard boundary derived from the location's latitude and date, not a designer's number.
- **Takes.** Blocks of 3-5 nights; prep 2-3 days ahead
- **Television.** An eight-day episode can rarely give more than two nights; a feature can give a two-week night block. This is one of the sharpest structural differences between the forms.

### Company move planning

Moves the whole unit from one location to another inside a shooting day. It costs an hour or two of daylight, a full unload and reset, and a fresh parking and basecamp footprint.

- **Who.** 1st AD schedules it; Transportation Coordinator and Location Manager execute
- **Decides.** Whether to move at all versus splitting the work across two days, when in the day to move (a lunch-time move is cheapest), and whether to run a partial move with a splinter unit.
- **Engine.** A move is a scheduled object with a duration derived from travel time, unit size, truck count and parking distance, plus a fixed re-establish cost. Two moves in one day should destroy that day's capacity in the model, because they do in life.
- **Takes.** 45 minutes to 3 hours depending on distance and unit size
- **Television.** TV budgets for at most one move a day and often none, because the day count is fixed; features will accept a bad move day to hold a location.

### Travel days and distant location

Schedules travel to and from a location outside the studio zone, with hotels, per diem, and the rule that travel days are paid and often count against the week.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Coordinator; 2nd AD marks the DOOD
- **Decides.** Whether a location is distant or local, whether to travel on a work day or a day off, and who travels when — cast staggered against crew.
- **Engine.** A studio-zone radius on the map that flips a location from local to distant, changing per diem, hotel, mileage, turnaround minima and whether travel counts as a work day. Travel must consume clock time and produce fatigue.
- **Takes.** 1 day per direction domestically; 1-2 days internationally plus a rest day
- **Television.** Series rarely go distant mid-season; features build whole schedules around distant blocks. A distant block in TV usually means a location episode planned a season ahead.

### Sixth-day scheduling

Adds a sixth consecutive working day to recover a lost day or fit a location window, at premium pay across the entire company.

- **Who.** UPM proposes; Line Producer and Producer approve; 1st AD builds it
- **Decides.** Whether to work six, which department heads are needed, and whether the recovered day is worth the premium and the fatigue.
- **Engine.** A sixth day multiplies the whole day's labour cost (commonly time-and-a-half — verify against current agreements), shortens the weekend rest, and compounds fatigue. The model must make repeated sixth days visibly degrade the next week, not just cost money.
- **Takes.** Decided a week ahead where possible; sometimes Thursday for Saturday
- **Television.** Network TV avoids sixth days because they break the episode rhythm and the next episode's prep; features use them to save a schedule.

### Seventh-day scheduling

Working a seventh consecutive day — rare, expensive, and a signal that the schedule has failed. Paid at the highest premium and with no rest before the following week.

- **Who.** Producer authorises; UPM executes
- **Decides.** Whether the production is in enough trouble to spend this, and whether the bond company or studio will even permit it.
- **Engine.** Double-time or higher across the company, a weekend-rest violation for everyone, and a standing hit with crew and guilds. A production that schedules seventh days repeatedly should start losing crew to other shows.
- **Takes.** Emergency decision
- **Television.** Effectively unavailable to episodic TV; the next episode's prep occupies the day.

### Holiday handling

Places recognised holidays on the board as non-working red strips, or schedules through them at premium pay when a location or actor window demands it.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Coordinator; 1st AD places them on the board
- **Decides.** Whether to shoot a holiday, whether to give a bridge day when a holiday lands mid-week, and which holidays the production recognises beyond the contractual list.
- **Engine.** A real holiday calendar per jurisdiction and per agreement, loaded from data rather than hard-coded, with premium multipliers and observed-date rules when a holiday falls at a weekend. The world clock must already know the holidays before the board is built.
- **Takes.** Set once in the calendar; revisited when a block shifts
- **Television.** TV schedules a formal hiatus around the winter holidays — commonly two weeks — which is a structural break in the season, not a day off.

### Cover set selection

Nominates an interior that is dressed, lit and ready to shoot at short notice, so a rained-out exterior can be replaced the same morning without losing the day.

- **Who.** 1st AD with the Production Designer and Location Manager
- **Decides.** Which set to hold in reserve, whether to pay to keep it standing and dressed, and whether the cover work is real scheduled work or a genuine spare.
- **Engine.** A cover set is a location with a readiness state, a holding cost per day, a cast requirement that must be satisfiable on short notice, and a swap latency. Holding a cover set must cost money continuously, or every production would hold one for free.
- **Takes.** Chosen at the board stage; readiness maintained all shoot
- **Television.** TV's standing sets are permanent cover, which is a large part of why TV can hold an eight-day schedule through a bad winter.

### Weather call

Decides at the last responsible moment whether today's exterior goes ahead, moves to cover, or is called off entirely with the crew paid.

- **Who.** 1st AD and UPM, typically before dawn, on a forecast and a paid meteorologist's read
- **Decides.** Go, cover, or scrub; and whether to make the call early enough to save the catering and crew calls or late enough to catch a clearing sky.
- **Engine.** A weather model with a forecast that is a noisy, improving estimate of a hidden actual, drawn from a named seeded stream so the same save replays the same storms. Calling early trades information for saved cost; calling late risks paying for a day that never shoots.
- **Takes.** The decision window is 04:00-06:00; the forecast is watched for a week
- **Television.** Same mechanic, tighter tolerance: a scrubbed day in an eight-day episode is a tenth of the schedule.

### Weather day and insurance recovery

Books the lost day, decides whether it is recoverable under the production's weather cover, and finds where in the schedule the lost work is rebuilt.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Accountant; the insurer's adjuster and the completion bond rep
- **Decides.** Whether to claim, whether to add a day at the end, whether to absorb by overloading other days, and whether to cut pages.
- **Engine.** An insurance policy object with named perils, a deductible in days or dollars, and a claims process that pays late. Recovery options must be genuinely different: added days cost the tail, overloading costs overtime and quality, cutting pages costs the film.
- **Takes.** Claim decided within days; recovery scheduled immediately
- **Television.** TV more often cuts pages or absorbs into the next episode's block because the air date cannot move.

### Daylight window planning

Schedules scenes against the real sun: sunrise, golden hour, sunset, civil twilight, and the direction the sun falls on a given wall at a given hour of a given date.

- **Who.** 1st AD with the DP; Script Supervisor tracks continuity of light
- **Decides.** Which scenes get the magic-hour slot (there are two short ones a day), which direction the company shoots first, and whether a scene is chased across two days for matching light.
- **Engine.** A solar position model from latitude, longitude, date and time, plus a per-location horizon and obstruction profile. Magic hour is a computed window of roughly 20-40 minutes depending on latitude and season, not a constant. Light continuity must be checkable across shoot days.
- **Takes.** Planned at the scout; recomputed for every date change
- **Television.** TV rarely chases magic hour; it costs a whole day's structure for a few minutes of footage.

### Child hours scheduling

Builds the day around a minor's legal limits: hours at the place of employment, work hours, schooling, and rest and recreation, all varying by age and whether school is in session.

- **Who.** 2nd AD and 2nd 2nd AD; Studio Teacher has statutory authority to stop the work
- **Decides.** Which scenes a minor is in on which day, whether to use a double or a stand-in, whether to shoot out of order to protect the child's hours, and whether to cast older.
- **Engine.** An age-banded statutory table by jurisdiction (California's bands run roughly 15 days-6 months, 6 months-2 years, 2-6, 6-9, 9-16, 16-18, with work time rising from 20 minutes to 6 hours — cite the statute, mark figures for verification). Teacher ratios, 12-hour rest, and a hard stop that the solver cannot cross.
- **Takes.** Continuous; recomputed every time a call moves
- **Television.** A series with a child regular shapes every episode around the table for years; the cumulative schooling requirement is tracked across the season, not the day.

### Studio teacher and schooling blocks

Books the teacher, schedules the schooling in legal blocks (typically no block shorter than twenty minutes, with a daily minimum), and certifies the minor's hours on the production report.

- **Who.** Studio Teacher (credentialed), booked by the Production Coordinator
- **Decides.** How many teachers to book against how many minors, where in the day to place school, and whether to shoot during a block and make it up later.
- **Engine.** Teacher-to-minor ratio rules (widely cited as one teacher per ten school-age minors — verify), a minimum-block rule that fragments the shooting day, and a daily schooling total that the day must actually contain. The teacher must be able to halt the day in the simulation.
- **Takes.** 3 hours of school on a school-in-session day, in blocks
- **Television.** Series hire a standing teacher; the child's academic record accumulates across the season and can fail, which should have consequences.

### Animal day scheduling

Schedules animal work around training, rest, heat, and the animal's genuine working limits, with the safety representative present and the script submitted in advance.

- **Who.** Head Animal Trainer or Wrangler; 1st AD schedules; American Humane Certified Animal Safety Representative attends
- **Decides.** Which animal, whether to use two lookalikes to double the working hours, whether to shoot the animal's work first, and whether to replace the action with VFX.
- **Engine.** Animals are elements with training lead time, daily working limits, temperature and weather thresholds, rest requirements, and handler ratios. The safety representative is a gating requirement: no representative, no 'No Animals Were Harmed' end credit and a real reputational cost.
- **Takes.** Weeks of training lead; animal work is front-loaded in the day
- **Television.** A series animal becomes a long-lived element with its own ageing, availability and eventual replacement.

### Stunt day scheduling

Schedules stunt sequences with their rehearsal, rigging, safety meeting, medic, and the day's other work arranged around a sequence that cannot be rushed.

- **Who.** Stunt Coordinator with the 1st AD; Producer approves the budget
- **Decides.** Rehearsal days, whether the stunt shoots on main unit or second unit, how many takes are realistically available, and whether to reduce the stunt or spend the day.
- **Engine.** Stunts carry rig time, rehearsal days, performer doubles, ratchet or vehicle equipment, a medic requirement, and a risk distribution over takes. Rushing a stunt must raise injury probability, which then costs schedule, insurance and standing.
- **Takes.** Rig 2-8 hours; rehearsal 1-3 days ahead; the stunt itself may be 2 takes
- **Television.** TV pushes stunts to second unit or splits them across a block; an eight-day episode cannot give a whole day to a rig.

### Practical and mechanical effects scheduling

Schedules rain, wind, snow, fire, squibs, breakaways and rigs, each of which needs build time, reset time between takes, and permits or officers on site.

- **Who.** Special Effects Coordinator with the 1st AD; Fire Safety Officer for pyro
- **Decides.** Practical or VFX, how many resets the day can afford, whether to shoot the effect last (because it destroys the set) or first, and whether to build a second copy.
- **Engine.** Effects carry build days, per-take reset time, consumable counts, permit requirements and safety officers. Reset time is the killer: a squib that resets in twenty minutes limits the day to a handful of takes, and the engine must enforce that.
- **Takes.** Build 1-10 days; reset 10 minutes to 2 hours per take
- **Television.** TV tends to buy one big effect per episode and schedule the rest of the block around its build.

### VFX plate, techvis and shoot-day scheduling

Schedules the on-set requirements of visual effects: plates, clean passes, lidar scans, witness cameras, HDRI captures, greenscreen setup time, and motion control passes.

- **Who.** VFX Supervisor and VFX Producer with the 1st AD
- **Decides.** Which shots get plates on the day versus a plate unit later, whether to shoot on an LED volume, and how much extra time each VFX setup buys itself.
- **Engine.** VFX shots are elements with on-set time costs (a clean pass is a real setup), downstream post cost, and a methodology choice that changes both. Volume stages change the weather and daylight constraints entirely and must reprice the day.
- **Takes.** Adds 15-60 minutes per VFX setup; plate units run separately for weeks
- **Television.** Episodic VFX counts are high and per-shot budgets low, so TV pushes toward volume stages and in-camera solutions that protect the day count.

### Special equipment booking

Books cranes, telescoping arms, remote heads, Steadicam, process trailers, camera cars, drones, underwater housings and rigging for the specific days they are needed.

- **Who.** 1st AD with the DP, Key Grip and Transportation Coordinator; UPM approves the rental
- **Decides.** Which days carry which equipment, whether to cluster all crane work onto one day to save rental days, and whether the equipment's setup time fits the day.
- **Engine.** Equipment is an element with a daily rate, minimum rental period, transport time, setup and strike time, an operator crew, and often a permit. Clustering crane work must genuinely save money in the ledger, which is why the board groups by equipment as well as location.
- **Takes.** Booked 1-3 weeks ahead; setup 1-4 hours on the day
- **Television.** Series keep a standing package and book specials by the episode; features build a package for the whole shoot.

### Stage booking and set turnover

Books stage space by the week, schedules which set occupies which stage when, and the turnover between striking one set and standing the next.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Coordinator with the lot's Stage Manager; Construction Coordinator schedules the build
- **Decides.** How many stages to hold, whether to build and strike or hold a set standing, and whether to pay for an extra stage to avoid a turnover that would stop the shoot.
- **Engine.** Stages are finite world resources with rental rates, dimensions, grid height, power and soundproofing, booked on a calendar that other productions compete for. Turnover is a scheduled, costed construction task that blocks shooting on that stage.
- **Takes.** Turnover 3-10 days per set; stages booked by the week or month
- **Television.** A series holds stages for a whole season; this is the lot's most reliable revenue and the single largest constraint on a new show's start date.

### Construction, dressing, pre-rig and strike scheduling

Schedules the work that must finish before a set can shoot: build, paint, dress, pre-light, pre-rig, and the strike that clears the space afterwards.

- **Who.** Construction Coordinator, Set Decorator, Leadman, Gaffer and Key Grip; UPM sequences them
- **Decides.** Build order across sets, whether to pre-rig a location the day before, whether to run a second dressing crew, and which sets can be dressed in the gap between units.
- **Engine.** Each set has a dependency chain (build finishes before dress starts, dress before pre-light, pre-light before shoot) with durations, crew sizes and costs. The board's first shoot day for a set is hard-constrained by the chain's finish date — this is the constraint most often modelled as decoration.
- **Takes.** Build 2-8 weeks; dress 2-5 days; pre-light 1-2 days; strike 1-3 days
- **Television.** Series swing gangs redress standing sets overnight between episodes; the turnover is measured in hours, not weeks, and it is a recurring line.

### Location permit and availability gating

Secures the permit, the neighbourhood notification, the parking, the police or fire officers, and the owner's window, each of which can veto a scheduled day.

- **Who.** Location Manager and Key Assistant Location Manager; Production Coordinator files
- **Decides.** Whether to permit or shoot guerrilla, which hours to request, how many notification days to allow, and whether to pay a location fee for a wider window.
- **Engine.** Permits have a jurisdiction, a lead time (in Los Angeles commonly around three business days for a standard permit — verify), a fee, required officers, hour restrictions and a refusal probability. A day without a valid permit must be blocked or it is not a rule.
- **Takes.** Lead time days to weeks; owner negotiation 1-4 weeks
- **Television.** Series build relationships with recurring locations and hold standing permits; features negotiate every location cold.

### Background counts per day

Sets how many background performers each scene needs, of which types, and how many are union, since background cost scales directly with the day's count.

- **Who.** 2nd 2nd AD and Background Casting Director; UPM signs the numbers
- **Decides.** Numbers per scene, union versus non-union mix, whether to reuse and re-costume the same performers across scenes, and whether to replace crowd with VFX.
- **Engine.** Background is an element with a per-head rate, a union count that triggers different rates once exceeded, costume and holding-area capacity, and a wrangling time cost. Doubling background must lengthen the day, not only the invoice.
- **Takes.** Set a week ahead; confirmed on the call sheet the day before
- **Television.** TV runs tighter background counts as a matter of house policy and uses the same faces repeatedly, which continuity must track.

### Meal break planning

Places the first meal within the contractual window from general crew call (commonly six hours) and the second meal after it, or takes the penalty.

- **Who.** 1st AD calls the breaks; UPM approves any waiver; caterer schedules against it
- **Decides.** Whether to break on time, take a grace period, run a walking meal, push the meal to hold a sequence, or run continuous with a buffet where the agreement permits.
- **Engine.** Meal clocks per person class, penalty accrual in escalating increments per half hour, and a second-meal rule. Penalties post to the ledger as real money. Working through a meal must buy schedule and cost cash, and the crew's fatigue must register it.
- **Takes.** 30-60 minutes; penalties accrue per half hour
- **Television.** Identical in rule; TV's compressed days make second meals more common on night and Friday work.

### Overtime and golden-time projection

Forecasts, from where the day stands at 3pm, what wrap will cost: which overtime tier the company enters, when double time begins, and when the day reaches golden time.

- **Who.** 1st AD projects during the day; UPM authorises; Production Accountant reconciles
- **Decides.** Whether to drop a scene, shorten coverage, split the remaining work to another day, or spend the overtime and finish.
- **Engine.** Escalating multipliers by elapsed hour and by agreement class, applied per person, projected forward from current progress. The projection must be visible to the player before the decision and must be wrong sometimes, because the day's remaining pace is uncertain.
- **Takes.** Projected hourly from mid-afternoon
- **Television.** Same mechanic; TV's hot-cost reporting to the studio makes the overrun visible to executives the next morning.

### One-liner schedule generation

Publishes the condensed schedule: one line per scene, grouped by shoot day, with day number, date, scene, INT/EXT, D/N, set, one-line description, pages and cast numbers.

- **Who.** 1st AD produces; 2nd AD distributes; everyone uses it
- **Decides.** Level of detail, whether to show cast names or numbers, whether to include location addresses, and how far ahead to publish.
- **Engine.** A pure render of the board — if the one-liner and the board can disagree, the document is a copy rather than a view. It must version and diff, because the whole crew reads the diff, not the document.
- **Takes.** Instant to generate; reissued on every revision
- **Television.** TV publishes a one-liner per episode and a block one-liner for cross-boarded days, which must reconcile.

### Full shooting schedule publication

Publishes the complete schedule: every breakdown sheet in shooting order, with all elements per day, totals, and the DOOD attached.

- **Who.** 1st AD produces; UPM and Line Producer approve; Producer and studio receive it
- **Decides.** When to publish (publishing early invites notes, publishing late invites chaos), and which version is the one the budget is locked against.
- **Engine.** A versioned publication with an author, a date, a colour or number, and an immutable snapshot. Departments order against a published version, so a later revision must produce a real change-order cost, not a silent overwrite.
- **Takes.** Published 2-4 weeks before the shoot; revised weekly and then daily
- **Television.** Published per episode against a season calendar; the season calendar is published once and defended all year.

### Schedule versioning and revision colours

Issues each revision on the next colour in the standard sequence so that a crew member can tell at a glance whether the paper in their hand is current.

- **Who.** Production Coordinator issues; 1st AD authors; Script Coordinator matches script colours
- **Decides.** When a change is big enough to warrant a reissue, whether to issue revised pages or a full document, and whether to hold changes for a weekly publication.
- **Engine.** An ordered colour sequence (white, blue, pink, yellow, green, goldenrod, buff, salmon, cherry, tan, then doubled — order varies by production and must be a configurable list), a revision date stamp, and asterisked changed lines. Old versions stay readable.
- **Takes.** Per revision; weekly in prep, sometimes daily in shoot
- **Television.** TV burns through colour sequences fast and often reaches double colours inside one episode; the sequence must wrap cleanly.

### Call sheet issue

Converts tomorrow's board day into individual calls: crew call, cast calls with makeup and wardrobe times, scenes and order, weather, sunrise and sunset, hospital, and the advance schedule.

- **Who.** 2nd AD writes it; 1st AD and UPM approve; Production Coordinator distributes
- **Decides.** Each performer's individual call (working backwards from their makeup and wardrobe time), the shooting order within the day, and what goes on the advance.
- **Engine.** Calls derive backwards from the first shot through hair, makeup, costume and travel durations per performer, constrained by that performer's turnaround. The call sheet must be generated from the board and the DOOD, never typed independently of them.
- **Takes.** Drafted mid-afternoon, approved at wrap, distributed by 8pm
- **Television.** Identical form; TV call sheets carry the episode number and often two episodes' scenes on cross-boarded days.

### Daily production report reconciliation

Records what actually happened: call and wrap times, scenes and pages completed, setups, meal times, penalties, and how far ahead or behind the production now stands.

- **Who.** 2nd AD compiles; UPM reviews; Production Accountant posts; studio and bond read it
- **Decides.** Nothing — it is the record. But it is the input to every subsequent decision the UPM makes.
- **Engine.** The report is the closing entry of a shoot day: actual against scheduled for pages, setups, hours and cost. It must feed back into the pace model so the next day's estimate learns. A production ahead or behind is a running scalar that drives producer pressure.
- **Takes.** Compiled during the day, closed within hours of wrap
- **Television.** TV reports roll up per episode and per block; being one day behind on episode three is a season-level problem.

### Rolling the board after a lost day

Rebuilds the remaining schedule after losing a day, which is never as simple as shifting everything one day: locations, actor windows, permits and turnaround all moved for their own reasons.

- **Who.** 1st AD rebuilds; UPM and Line Producer decide the recovery
- **Decides.** Whether to shift, absorb, add a day at the end, work a sixth day, split a unit, or cut pages.
- **Engine.** Re-running the solver against a partially executed board, with executed days frozen and the remainder re-solved under updated constraints. The solver must present distinct, priced options rather than one answer, because the choice is the game.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours to rebuild; a day to get approvals
- **Television.** TV's recovery options are narrower: the next episode's prep and stage turnover leave no tail to extend into.

### Absorbing script revisions into the board

Re-tags, re-measures and re-strips only the scenes a revision touched, then tests whether the board still holds — new elements may need lead time the schedule no longer has.

- **Who.** 1st AD re-breaks the changed scenes; Script Coordinator issues the pages
- **Decides.** Whether to accept the revision as written, ask for it to be rewritten to fit the schedule, or spend to accommodate it.
- **Engine.** Scene-level dirty propagation: a changed scene invalidates its sheet, its strip, its elements' lead times, and its cast's DOOD cells, and nothing else. A new element whose lead time exceeds the days remaining must be reported as impossible, with a price to make it possible.
- **Takes.** 1-4 hours per revision; a page-one rewrite restarts the board
- **Television.** Constant in TV, where scripts revise during the shoot. The father-as-showrunner case makes this a direct conflict between two of the player's own roles.

### Budget and schedule feedback

Prices the board and feeds the number back into the ordering: day count times day rate, plus every element's rental days, holds, moves, nights, and penalties the board implies.

- **Who.** Line Producer and UPM with the 1st AD; Producer arbitrates
- **Decides.** Whether to lose days, add days, trade a location for a build, recast to free a window, or cut scenes to make the number.
- **Engine.** A schedule-derived budget where changing the board changes the number immediately and correctly. Every scheduling object must carry cost: a move, a night, a hold day, a sixth day, a turnaround violation. A board with no price attached is the failure mode this whole system exists to avoid.
- **Takes.** Recomputed continuously; formal budget passes weekly in prep
- **Television.** TV budgets per episode against a pattern budget, with amortised costs for standing sets and series regulars spread across the season.

### Incentive-constrained scheduling

Shapes the board to qualify for a jurisdiction's rebate: minimum local shoot days, qualified local spend, local hire counts, and sometimes a required percentage of principal photography in the territory.

- **Who.** Line Producer and the production's tax incentive consultant; UPM implements
- **Decides.** Where to shoot, how many days to place in the incentive jurisdiction, whether to relocate a sequence, and whether the rebate beats the added travel.
- **Engine.** Jurisdictions as world objects with qualification thresholds, rebate percentages, caps, application deadlines and audit requirements. Failing a threshold by one day must forfeit the whole rebate, which is what makes the constraint bite.
- **Takes.** Decided before the board is built; audited after wrap
- **Television.** Series commit to a jurisdiction for years; the incentive decision is made once and constrains every season after.

### Completion bond and studio schedule approval

Reviews the board and budget for feasibility, sets the contingency, and retains the right to take over the production if it falls far enough behind.

- **Who.** Completion Guarantor's representative and the studio's production executive; Line Producer presents
- **Decides.** Whether to accept the bond's notes, which contingency percentage to carry, and how much schedule slack to show versus hold back.
- **Engine.** An external reviewer with its own model of feasibility that can reject a board. Falling behind by a defined margin must trigger escalating intervention: notes, then a representative on set, then takeover. This is the antagonist the schedule has.
- **Takes.** Review 1-2 weeks; approval before start of photography
- **Television.** Networks and streamers perform the same function through the production executive rather than a bond, with hot costs as the weekly trigger.

### Cross-boarding (concurrent episodes)

Shoots scenes from two or more episodes on the same day because they share a location or a set, collapsing what would otherwise be two visits into one.

- **Who.** 1st AD of the block, with both episodes' directors and the showrunner
- **Decides.** Which episodes board together, which director covers which scenes, and whether the efficiency is worth the directors' loss of control over their own episodes.
- **Engine.** The board's unit of scheduling stops being one script. Strips carry an episode id, the DOOD spans episodes, cast guarantees count per episode, and each director's days must be tracked separately against DGA minimums.
- **Takes.** Planned at block prep; typical blocks pair two episodes
- **Television.** This process does not exist in features at all. It is the single clearest reason television scheduling is not film scheduling with more episodes.

### Block structure and director rotation (TV)

Divides the season into blocks, assigns directors to each, and staggers prep, shoot and post so that one director preps while another shoots and a third is in the edit.

- **Who.** Showrunner, Producing Director and UPM; the season's Production Manager builds the calendar
- **Decides.** Block boundaries, which director gets which episodes, how much prep each gets, and whether to cross-board within the block.
- **Engine.** A rolling pipeline with overlapping stages per episode and shared finite resources (stages, cast, crew, the producing director's attention). Directors have guild-minimum prep and shoot days (commonly cited as roughly 7 prep and 8 shoot for a network hour — verify against the DGA Basic Agreement).
- **Takes.** The whole season calendar; blocks of 2 episodes over roughly 16 shooting days
- **Television.** Entirely a television process. Feature equivalent is second unit, which is not the same thing.

### Second and splinter unit scheduling

Runs a parallel unit for inserts, plates, establishing shots, stunts and crowd, so that main unit's day is spent on performance.

- **Who.** Second Unit Director with a second 1st AD; UPM allocates crew; main unit's 1st AD coordinates
- **Decides.** Whether to run a second unit at all, for how many days, with what crew size, and which work to hand it.
- **Engine.** A second unit is a full parallel board: its own strips, its own call, its own crew draw from a shared pool, its own equipment bookings. Splitting crew must weaken main unit. Shared actors cannot be on both units at once and their turnaround is shared.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks, often overlapping main unit or running after wrap
- **Television.** TV second units are short and surgical, typically a day or two per block; feature second units can run for months on large pictures.

### Insert, pickup and reshoot day scheduling

Schedules the days that fix what the edit revealed: inserts, missing coverage, additional dialogue scenes, and reshoots ordered after a test screening.

- **Who.** UPM and 1st AD; Post Production Supervisor supplies the list; Producer funds it
- **Decides.** Whether to schedule them into the tail of the shoot, hold a contingency block, or bring the company back months later at a far higher cost.
- **Engine.** Post must be able to generate a scheduling demand that the production model then has to satisfy: sets may be struck, actors may have changed appearance or be unavailable, and locations may be gone. The cost of a late pickup must scale with how much has been released.
- **Takes.** 1-10 days; reshoots months after wrap
- **Television.** TV runs pickup days inside blocks or during the next block's prep; a reshoot after air is not possible, which is why TV's notes come earlier.

### Rehearsal, fitting, test and table read scheduling

Schedules the paid days before the shoot: table read, rehearsal, wardrobe fittings, makeup and prosthetic tests, camera and lens tests, and stunt rehearsal.

- **Who.** 2nd AD books cast; Costume Supervisor books fittings; 1st AD schedules camera tests
- **Decides.** How many rehearsal days to buy, whether fittings are paid separately, and whether to spend a camera test day on the schedule or absorb it into prep.
- **Engine.** These days consume cast availability and appear in the DOOD as R and T codes with their own pay rules. A production that skips rehearsal and tests should shoot slower and make more mistakes on day one, or skipping costs nothing.
- **Takes.** Table read 3-4 hours; fittings 2-4 hours per performer; camera test 1 day
- **Television.** Multi-camera comedy makes rehearsal the spine of the week — table read, rewrite, blocking, camera blocking, shoot night — which is a fundamentally different schedule shape from anything in film.

### Prep ladder and department start dates

Sets when each department starts being paid, working backwards from the first shoot day through the lead time each needs to be ready.

- **Who.** UPM and Line Producer; each department head negotiates their own start
- **Decides.** Who starts when — every week of early start is real money, every week too late is a department that is not ready on day one.
- **Engine.** Each department has a required lead time that is a function of the board's demands: construction's start derives from the largest build, costume's from the number of costumes and builds, transport's from the vehicle count. Starting late must produce a real failure on the day, not a warning.
- **Takes.** Features 8-16 weeks of prep; low budget 4-6
- **Television.** TV departments start once per season and run continuously; the ladder is built for episode one and then becomes a rhythm.

### Wrap-out scheduling

Schedules the strike, the returns, the location restorations, the asset disposal or storage, and the paperwork that closes the production after the last shot.

- **Who.** UPM and Production Coordinator; department heads wrap their own
- **Decides.** What to store against a sequel or a next season, what to sell, what to strike immediately to stop rental clocks, and how many wrap days each department gets.
- **Engine.** Rental clocks keep running until returns are logged, so a slow wrap must keep costing money. Stored assets become a company-level inventory that a future production can draw on — which is where the mother's production company and the father's lot both have a standing interest.
- **Takes.** 1-4 weeks after wrap; construction strike can run longer
- **Television.** A renewed series does not wrap; it hiatuses, holding stages and assets at a reduced rate. A cancelled series wraps hard and the lot reclaims the stages.

### Option spaces

**Element categories (the tagging schema)** — 45 options

`Cast` · `Background / Atmosphere` · `Silent Bits` · `Stand-Ins` · `Stunts` · `Stunt Doubles` · `Photo Doubles` · `Picture Vehicles` · `Production Vehicles` · `Props` · `Set Dressing` · `Greenery` · `Wardrobe` · `Makeup` · `Hair` · `Prosthetics` · `Special Effects (mechanical / practical)` · `Visual Effects` · `Optical FX` · `Animals` · `Animal Wrangler` · `Livestock` · `Music (playback / on-camera)` · `Sound (playback / effects)` · `Camera (special)` · `Special Equipment` · `Special Grip` · `Special Electric` · `Art Department` · `Construction` · `Security` · `Additional Labor` · `Drones / Aerial` · `Marine / Water` · `Aircraft` · `Weapons / Armourer` · `Fire / Pyrotechnics` · `Medic / Nurse` · `Intimacy Coordinator` · `Choreographer` · `Dialect Coach` · `Studio Teacher` · `Location Notes` · `Miscellaneous` · `Notes`

`Red — cast (speaking roles)` · `Blue — background / atmosphere` · `Green — background with silent bits` · `Yellow — stunts` · `Orange — special effects` · `Purple — props, vehicles and animals (production-defined)` · `Box — sound effects and music` · `Circle — wardrobe, makeup and hair` · `Straight underline — special equipment` · `Asterisk or squiggle — production notes`

**Strip colours** — 9 options

`White — INT DAY` · `Yellow — EXT DAY` · `Blue — INT NIGHT (Movie Magic default; the generic convention puts blue on EXT NIGHT)` · `Green — EXT NIGHT (Movie Magic default; the generic convention puts green on INT NIGHT)` · `Black — day break` · `Red — holiday` · `Grey — free / idle day` · `Banner (white text bar) — company move, travel, unit split, note` · `Production-defined colour — dawn, dusk, magic hour`

**Cast engagement types** — 16 options

`Day Performer` · `Three-Day Performer (television only)` · `Weekly Performer` · `Run of Show / Run of Picture` · `Series Regular` · `Guest Star` · `Co-Star` · `Recurring` · `Background (union)` · `Background (non-union)` · `Stunt Performer` · `Stunt Double` · `Photo Double` · `Stand-In` · `Voice / Looping` · `Minor (any of the above, plus statutory limits)`

**Day types on the board** — 21 options

`Shooting day` · `Travel day` · `Prep day` · `Rehearsal day` · `Camera / wardrobe test day` · `Company move day` · `Hold day (paid)` · `Idle day (unpaid)` · `Weather day` · `Weather cover day` · `Holiday` · `Day off` · `Sixth day` · `Seventh day` · `Second unit day` · `Splinter unit day` · `Plate unit day` · `Insert day` · `Pickup / reshoot day` · `Hiatus day (TV)` · `Turnover day (stage)`

**Scheduling objectives the player weights** — 15 options

`Fewest total shooting days` · `Fewest company moves` · `Fewest location visits (never return to a location)` · `Cheapest total cost` · `Shortest actor holds` · `Protect the star's window` · `Most favourable weather odds for exteriors` · `Fewest night blocks` · `Best light for the biggest scenes` · `Shoot in story order where possible` · `Shoot the hardest work first` · `Shoot the hardest work last (after the company is fast)` · `Protect child hours` · `Maximise incentive-qualified days` · `Leave contingency in the tail`

**Unit types** — 11 options

`Main unit` · `Second unit` · `Splinter unit` · `Plate unit` · `Aerial unit` · `Underwater / marine unit` · `Motion control unit` · `Stunt unit` · `Background / crowd unit` · `Insert unit` · `Visual effects data unit (lidar, HDRI, witness)`

**Cover set kinds** — 7 options

`Standing stage set, dressed and lit` · `Interior at the same location as the lost exterior` · `Dry-for-wet or interior-for-exterior substitution` · `Insert and pickup list held in reserve` · `Second unit work brought forward` · `Rehearsal or camera test converted to a working day` · `No cover — release the company and take the loss`

**Call patterns** — 10 options

`Standard day call (crew call at first light or a fixed morning hour)` · `Sunrise call` · `Split call (afternoon into night)` · `Night call` · `Sunset / magic hour call` · `Staggered call (departments in waves)` · `Pre-call (specific departments early)` · `Weather-permitting call with a decision deadline` · `Will-notify (call issued the night before)` · `Continuous / French hours (where the agreement permits or is waived)`

**Turnaround regimes** — 7 options

`Performer rest period (commonly 12 hours in the studio zone; shorter on overnight location — verify against the current SAG-AFTRA Codified Basic Agreement)` · `Crew daily rest (10 hours under the IATSE Basic Agreement from 2021 onward — verify)` · `Weekend rest (54 hours on a five-day week, shorter on a six-day week — verify)` · `Minor's rest (12 hours between dismissal and next call)` · `Distant-location variant` · `Negotiated waiver` · `Forced call (breach, paid as a penalty)`

**Recognised holidays (US production)** — 12 options

`New Year's Day` · `Martin Luther King Jr. Day` · `Presidents' Day` · `Memorial Day` · `Juneteenth` · `Independence Day` · `Labor Day` · `Thanksgiving Day` · `Day after Thanksgiving` · `Christmas Day` · `New Year's Eve (production-defined)` · `Production-declared day (a wrap day, a release day)`

`Sound stage (by size and grid height)` · `Insert stage` · `LED volume stage` · `Water tank / dive tank` · `Exterior backlot street` · `Standing set (series-owned)` · `Practical location (private)` · `Practical location (public / municipal)` · `Studio-owned ranch or lot exterior` · `Distant location (outside the studio zone)` · `Process / camera car` · `Aerial platform`

**Special equipment day types** — 16 options

`Technocrane / telescoping crane` · `Conventional crane or jib` · `Remote head` · `Steadicam` · `Cable cam` · `Dolly track (extended)` · `Process trailer` · `Camera car / pursuit vehicle` · `Drone / aerial platform` · `Helicopter` · `Underwater housing and dive crew` · `Motion control rig` · `Ratchet / descender / wire rig` · `Condor lifts` · `Rain towers, wind machines, snow rigs` · `Additional camera bodies (B, C, D)`

**Schedule documents published** — 14 options

`Preliminary schedule (prep)` · `Stripboard / production board` · `One-liner schedule` · `Full shooting schedule with breakdown sheets` · `Day Out of Days` · `Call sheet` · `Advance schedule (next three days)` · `Weekly schedule` · `Daily production report` · `Hot costs` · `Cast deal memo / start slip` · `Location and permit calendar` · `Construction and dressing schedule` · `Second unit schedule`

**Day-sizing targets by format** — 9 options

`Studio feature: 2-3 pages a day over 40-80 shooting days` · `Mid-budget feature: 4-5 pages a day over 30-40 days` · `Low-budget feature: 6-8 pages a day over 18-24 days` · `Network hour drama: 5-6 pages a day over 8 shooting days an episode` · `Streaming hour drama: 9-12 shooting days an episode` · `Half-hour single camera: 4-5 shooting days an episode` · `Multi-camera comedy: a full episode in one audience night at the end of a five-day rehearsal week` · `Daytime serial: 60-100 pages a day` · `Commercial / short form: 1-3 days total`

**Meal regimes** — 8 options

`Six-hour first meal, standard sit-down` · `Walking meal` · `Grace period taken (short paid extension before penalty)` · `Second meal after the second six-hour period` · `Meal penalty accepted (escalating per half hour)` · `Continuous shooting with a running buffet (French hours), where permitted or waived` · `Separate cast and crew meal times` · `Catered on location versus a nearby facility`

**Animal action classes** — 10 options

`Animal present, no action` · `Simple trained action` · `Complex trained action` · `Animal with a performer in contact` · `Animal in a moving vehicle` · `Animal with fire, water, height or weapons` · `Animal simulated by puppet, prosthetic or VFX` · `Livestock in numbers` · `Wild or exotic species` · `Insects, reptiles and fish`

**Constraint hardness** — 7 options

`Statutory hard (child hours, permit hours, curfew)` · `Contractual hard (actor availability window, turnaround, guarantee)` · `Physical hard (set not built, location does not exist yet, sun has set)` · `Financial hard (bond cap, incentive threshold)` · `Strong soft (star's preference, director's request, weather odds)` · `Weak soft (department convenience, catering)` · `Aspirational (story order, shooting the hard scenes last)`

**Weather call outcomes** — 9 options

`Shoot as scheduled` · `Shoot with weather mitigation (rain towers reversed, tents, covered sets)` · `Move to cover set` · `Move to second unit work` · `Delay the call and reassess` · `Shoot half a day and release` · `Scrub and pay the day` · `Scrub and claim on insurance` · `Shoot it wet and rewrite the scene as rain`

**Television block structures** — 8 options

`One director, one episode, sequential` · `Two-episode blocks, cross-boarded` · `Three-episode blocks (limited series and streaming)` · `Whole-season single director (limited series)` · `Producing director shooting the pattern episodes` · `Split block (one director shoots the A story, another the B)` · `Second unit absorbing one episode's action across the season` · `Multi-camera weekly rehearsal cycle`

**Company move types** — 8 options

`No move (single location day)` · `Short move within a location (walk the equipment)` · `Lunch-time move (cheapest, absorbs into the break)` · `Full mid-day move` · `Move to basecamp only (cast and crew, not equipment)` · `Overnight move (trucks move after wrap)` · `Distant move with travel day` · `Splinter move (part of the unit moves while the rest shoots)`

**Who the player schedules as** — 4 options

`As the actor — reading the DOOD and the call sheet, negotiating availability, pushing for a start date, refusing a forced call` · `As the mother (production company) — approving the board, funding days, choosing the incentive jurisdiction, hiring the line producer` · `As the father (lot and production side, and the writer) — allocating stages, setting turnover, and writing the revisions the board then has to absorb` · `As none of them — watching a schedule you do not control happen to you`

### What gets got wrong

- Page eighths are not time. A two-eighth dialogue scene with six setups takes longer than a two-page walk-and-talk shot as a oner. If the engine sizes the day on eighths it will be wrong in exactly the way inexperienced schedules are wrong — which is a fine thing to simulate for a novice 1st AD, but the engine still needs the real number underneath.
- Strip colour is derived, never chosen. So is the Day Out of Days. Any UI that lets the player set them directly has turned two of the domain's most legible outputs into decoration. The right interaction is: change the board, watch the colours and the DOOD change.
- A hold day costs a full day's salary for a weekly performer and nothing for an idle day performer. If holds are free in the model, the whole point of building a board around cast availability disappears and every schedule converges on story order.
- Turnaround is per person, not per day. A single actor wrapping at 2am does not push the whole company's call — it pushes that actor's call, which may or may not be the first shot. Modelling it as a day-level flag removes the most interesting scheduling decisions in the domain.
- Company moves, night walks and turnaround interact. Walking to nights at two to three hours a day is a consequence of the rest period, not a convention. If turnaround is modelled correctly the walk emerges by itself; if it is not, the walk has to be faked with a rule, and the rule will be wrong at the edges.
