- Construction and dressing are hard predecessors of the first shoot day on a set, and this is the constraint most often modelled as a warning label. If a production can schedule a set before it is built and merely see a red icon, the art department has no teeth and the build schedule is fiction.
- Television's unit of scheduling is not the script. Cross-boarding puts two episodes on one day; blocks put three directors in three different stages of the same pipeline. An engine that assumes one script maps to one board cannot represent episodic television at all.
- The one-liner, the full schedule, the DOOD and the call sheet are four views of one dataset. Build them as four documents and they will drift, and the player will learn that documents in this game do not mean anything.
- California minor work hours vary by age band, by whether school is in session, and by the distinction between time at the place of employment and time actually working. The table must be loaded as verified statutory data. The figures in this document are from general industry knowledge and were not re-verified in session — verify against the California Labor Code and Title 8 CCR before shipping.
- Union figures move every contract cycle. Rest periods, meal penalties, overtime tiers, sixth- and seventh-day multipliers and holiday lists all change with each SAG-AFTRA, IATSE and DGA agreement. These belong in a dated, versioned data file with the agreement and year attached, not as constants in code — and a game set across years should let them change mid-save.
- The forecast must be a noisy view of a hidden truth, drawn from a named seeded stream. If the player can see the real weather, the weather call — one of the genuinely hard decisions in the domain — becomes arithmetic.
- Being ahead or behind is a running scalar, not a binary. One day behind on day five of a feature is recoverable; one day behind on day five of an eight-day episode is the whole block. The same number must mean different things in the two forms, and the bond company or the studio executive must react to it.
- Drop-and-pickup has a contractual minimum gap and cannot be used to solve every hold problem. If the engine permits an unconstrained drop, the optimal schedule becomes a comb of one-day engagements that no real production could ever run.
- Scheduling is a constraint-satisfaction problem with a cost objective, not a sort. Any implementation that orders strips by a single key will produce boards that look plausible and price wrong, and the price is the whole game.
- Every scheduling object must carry cost, or the board is a picture. A move, a night, a hold, a sixth day, a turnaround breach, a meal penalty, a stage turnover, a permit, a weather day: each needs a real number that posts to the ledger, which is the only way changing one input a little changes the outcome rather than the prose.
- Second unit is not free capacity. It draws from the same crew pool, the same equipment and the same cast. If the engine lets a second unit be conjured at a flat cost, every schedule problem is solved by adding units.
- The AD's shot estimate is the number the whole schedule stands on, and it is a judgement. Model it as a learned per-director, per-DP pace rather than a constant, and let the player be wrong about it — a director who says twenty setups and shoots twelve is the most common way a schedule dies.
- Omitted scenes must keep their numbers and A-scenes must sort between integers. An engine that renumbers on revision will break every document the crew is holding, which is exactly what the lock exists to prevent.

---

## 6. Budgeting, financing and the bond

*feature film and scripted television, from first script breakdown through final cost report, incentive audit and the waterfall*

### Budget script breakdown

Every scene is tagged for cast, background, vehicles, animals, stunts, SFX, VFX, props, wardrobe, set dressing, greens, livestock, minors, and location type. Tags become the quantity side of every below-the-line account.

- **Who.** Line Producer, with the 1st AD and Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Which elements get tagged, whether each is built, rented, borrowed, cheated or cut, and how hard to push the director to combine or lose them. As the mother (producer) this is where the picture's real size is set.
- **Engine.** Scene records holding typed element lists; an element-kind -> account map; per-element unit cost, unit count and duration; recompute that pushes a tag change straight to account totals. Untag the stunt and the stunt coordinator's prep days, the pad rental and the 20% stunt fringe all vanish.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days for a feature; 4-12 hours per episode in television
- **Television.** Broken down per episode against a standing pattern, so only the deltas price out: a recurring set is already paid for, a new one is an above-pattern hit that has to be approved separately.

### Stripboard and Day Out of Days

Scenes are ordered into shooting days by location, cast, unit, day/night and company move. The DOOD table falls out of it: every actor's Start, Work, Hold, Travel, Idle and Finish days.

- **Who.** First Assistant Director
- **Decides.** Total shoot days, shoot order, how long each actor is held, whether to drop-and-pick-up an actor, how many company moves, and how many units run at once.
- **Engine.** Ordered day objects with per-day cast call sets; DOOD derived from the board, never typed; SWHTF codes; a cost function that prices hold days, consecutive-employment rules and drop/pickup minimums out of the actual guild agreement, not a flat day rate.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days to a first board; revised daily thereafter
- **Television.** Cross-boarding two or three episodes at once means one shooting day belongs to several episodes and its cost must be split by scene count, page count or a negotiated key. Get the split rule wrong and every per-episode number is wrong.

### Chart of accounts selection and account numbering

A numbered account tree is adopted — studio chart, Movie Magic/EP default, broadcaster template or financier-mandated — and every line is given a 4-digit account plus a 2-digit detail code (e.g. 3300-01 Director of Photography).

- **Who.** Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Which chart, how deep the sub-accounts run, and which cost sits in which account — a decision that later determines what a tax credit auditor will and will not allow.
- **Engine.** Account tree: number, title, class (ATL/BTL/post/other), fringe-eligible flag, incentive-qualifying flag per jurisdiction, parent chain to the top sheet. Move a cost from 3600 Location to 2200 Art and its incentive treatment and its fringe base both change.
- **Takes.** 1 day, but it must be locked before the first purchase order is cut
- **Television.** Television adds an episode dimension and amortization accounts, so every posting carries account plus episode number plus pattern/above-pattern flag. Three coordinates, not one.

### Top sheet assembly

Account totals roll into four blocks — Above the Line, Production (Below the Line), Post Production, Other — then Total Above, Total Below, Total Above and Below, Contingency, Completion Bond, and Grand Total.

- **Who.** Line Producer
- **Decides.** Where the picture's money goes proportionally: the ATL/BTL ratio, how much of the negative is cast, how much is on the screen.
- **Engine.** Pure rollup from the account tree, never hand-entered. Subtotal lines are computed. Change one day rate anywhere and the grand total moves, or the top sheet is a picture of a budget rather than a budget.
- **Takes.** Continuous; a fresh top sheet in minutes once the detail exists
- **Television.** Two top sheets: pattern (the typical episode) and season (pattern x episode count + amortized + above-pattern). Neither alone tells you what the season costs.

### Above-the-line quoting and negotiation

Story and rights, producers, director, writers and cast are priced from quotes, precedent and the guild minimums. Pay-or-play commitments are made here and cannot be unmade.

- **Who.** Producer and Business Affairs, against agents and the guilds
- **Decides.** Who to attach, at what number, with what backend, and whether to pay a quote or go to the next name down. The player-as-actor sits on the other side of this table.
- **Engine.** Person records carrying last quote, quote floor, agent aggression, willingness to take scale+10, backend appetite and a pay-or-play flag. An offer is an object with a response model that reads the part, the director, the money and the actor's typecasting state.
- **Takes.** Weeks to months; runs in parallel with everything else
- **Television.** Series regulars get an episodic fee, a 10-out-of-13 or 13-out-of-13 guarantee, per-season escalators and holding fees between seasons. A feature cast deal is a lump; a series cast deal is an annuity with options the studio holds.

### Cast account build (1400)

Principals, day players, stunt performers, stunt adjustments, looping/ADR, overtime allowance, travel and per diem are priced against the DOOD and the applicable SAG-AFTRA agreement.

- **Who.** Line Producer with the Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Scale versus overscale, weekly versus daily engagement, whether to buy out a performer's overtime, per diem level, travel class, and how much overage allowance to carry.
- **Engine.** Union agreement as data: minimum day and weekly rates by agreement tier, 6th/7th-day multipliers, overnight-location premiums, stunt adjustment bands, ADR day rules. Switch the agreement tier and the cast total must move by a real amount, not a label.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days to build, revised with every casting change
- **Television.** Guest stars and co-stars are budgeted per episode against the pattern; series regulars are amortized. Top-of-show rates create a hard ceiling that resets each season.

### Fringe table construction

A fringe is built per labour category: guild pension and health, employer FICA/OASDI and Medicare, FUTA, state unemployment, employment training tax, workers' compensation by class code, and the payroll company's handling fee.

- **Who.** Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Union footing, which fringes apply to which accounts, what workers' comp class codes the crew sits in, and whether box rentals and per diem are fringed (they should not be).
- **Engine.** Fringe objects with rate, base account set, annual ceiling and floor. OASDI 6.2% employer stops at $184,500 (2026); Medicare 1.45% never stops; FUTA 0.6% net on the first $7,000. Because of the ceilings the effective fringe rate FALLS as an individual's wages rise — model the ceiling or the whole thing is a flat multiplier pretending to be a system.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days to build; recalculated on every budget pass
- **Television.** Same mechanics, but a 22-episode order runs crew past every annual ceiling mid-season, so the effective fringe rate drops across the season. A per-episode flat fringe percentage is wrong by the back half.

### Union footing decision

The production signs (or does not sign) with SAG-AFTRA, DGA, WGA, IATSE and Teamsters, and picks a tier within each — low budget, modified low, ultra low, new media, Area Standards, Tier 1-3.

- **Who.** Producer, with production counsel
- **Decides.** Minimum rates, turnaround, meal penalties, forced calls, crew size requirements, residuals liability, and whether the picture can hire the actors it wants at all under Global Rule One.
- **Engine.** Signatory status as a capability gate, not a cosmetic field: it must block casting a union actor, change every minimum in the rate library, switch residual liability on, and add pension/health to the fringe table. Falsifiable by a sweep across tiers moving the grand total.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks for signatory paperwork; the decision itself is one meeting
- **Television.** Television carries its own SAG-AFTRA, WGA and DGA agreements with separate minimums, and streaming has separate residual and success-bonus structures from broadcast. A show that moves platform changes its liabilities.

### Labour rate build and the overtime model

Crew are priced at an hourly or daily rate with a guaranteed number of hours, then loaded with time-and-a-half, double time, golden time, sixth and seventh day, meal penalties, forced calls and turnaround violations.

- **Who.** Unit Production Manager
- **Decides.** Guaranteed hours (10, 11, 12), whether to buy out overtime with a flat weekly, camera crew size, how much overtime allowance to carry in the budget, and whether to run a 5-day or 6-day week.
- **Engine.** An hours-per-day distribution, not an average: a day that runs 14.2 hours must produce the exact premium stack. Meal penalty accrues in increments after the 6th hour from last meal. Turnaround violation is a per-person penalty. These are the single largest source of below-the-line variance.
- **Takes.** 2-4 days; re-costed after every schedule change
- **Television.** Episodic shows run long weeks repeatedly, so turnaround and 6th-day penalties compound across a season in a way a 40-day feature never sees. French hours and Fraturdays are season-long cost problems.

### Vendor quoting and equipment package negotiation

Camera, lighting, grip, sound, vehicles, expendables and specialty gear are bid by two or three houses, then negotiated to a package rate — typically a weekly rate charged for a 3-day week.

- **Who.** UPM with department heads (DP, Gaffer, Key Grip, Costume Designer)
- **Decides.** Which rental house, package versus a la carte, how many weeks of prep and wrap are charged, loss-and-damage exposure, and whether to buy rather than rent.
- **Engine.** Vendor records with list rate, discount policy, relationship score, availability calendar and a weekly-for-3-days convention. Lengthen the shoot by a week and the rental total must step, not scale linearly, because rentals bill by week.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks of quoting during prep
- **Television.** Series take annual or season deals with a long-term discount, and carry gear across hiatus. The negotiation is a term deal, not a picture deal.

### Construction and art department bid

Set drawings are priced into labour hours by trade, materials, hardware, paint, greens and strike. A mill rate and a crew of a stated size over a stated number of weeks.

- **Who.** Construction Coordinator and Production Designer, approved by the Line Producer
- **Decides.** Build versus location versus virtual production, set count, whether a set is a wild wall or a full build, and how much of it strikes or stores.
- **Engine.** Set objects with square footage, trade-hour coefficients, material cost, build weeks, strike cost and a store/scrap flag. Storage creates a recurring monthly liability, so deciding to keep a set is a decision with a cash tail.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks to a costed bid; construction runs 4-12 weeks
- **Television.** Standing sets are built once and amortized across the order or across multiple seasons; swing sets are per-episode. A show's cost structure is largely how many standing sets it has.

### Location budget and permitting

Site fees, permit fees, police and fire officers, parking, basecamp, holding, impact payments to neighbours, cleanup and restoration, and location scout/manager labour.

- **Who.** Location Manager
- **Decides.** Practical location versus stage versus backlot, which jurisdiction to shoot in, how many company moves, and whether to pay an impact fee or move.
- **Engine.** Location records with day fee, permit regime, required safety officers, parking capacity, distance from the base (which drives mileage, travel time and distant-location per diem) and a neighbour tolerance that can force a shutdown.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks of scouting and permitting in prep
- **Television.** Recurring exteriors need a season-long agreement and a repeat-visit rate; a series that keeps returning to one street negotiates once and books repeatedly.

### Stage, lot and facility booking

Stages, mill space, production offices, parking, standing sets, backlot, post rooms and services are booked at a day or weekly rate, often with a four-wall or production services agreement.

- **Who.** UPM booking against the Studio Operations / lot management
- **Decides.** Which lot, which stages, how many prep weeks before and wrap weeks after the shoot, and whether to take a bundled services deal or four-wall and bring in your own everything.
- **Engine.** Stage inventory with dimensions, clear height, grid capacity, power, soundproofing, day rate, occupancy calendar and a conflicting-tenant model. Booking removes capacity from the lot's own P&L. For the father-as-lot-owner this is revenue on one side of the same ledger.
- **Takes.** Booked in prep; blocks held months ahead for series
- **Television.** A returning series holds its stages year-round including hiatus, paying to keep standing sets up. That hold is the lot's most valuable and most inflexible revenue.

### Transportation budget

Drivers, captains, picture vehicles, trucks, trailers, honeywagons, campers, fuel, mileage, tolls, vehicle prep and maintenance, and out-of-town relocation.

- **Who.** Transportation Coordinator
- **Decides.** Fleet size, how many drivers, whether cast get individual trailers or a two-banger, and whether crew drive themselves or are driven.
- **Engine.** Vehicle records with day rate, fuel consumption, required driver classification and a driver-to-vehicle ratio. Adding a location day adds drivers, fuel and a 12-hour day of Teamster labour with its own overtime rules.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks to build; adjusts with every schedule change
- **Television.** Season-long fleet leases rather than picture rentals; a fixed base and a repeated commute produce a predictable weekly number.

### Post-production budget build

Editorial crew and weeks, cutting rooms, dailies, media management, sound editorial, ADR, Foley, mix stage days, colour/DI, conform, titles, deliverables, QC, archive and masters.

- **Who.** Post Production Supervisor
- **Decides.** Number of editors and assistants, how many weeks of editorial, mix stage class, DI facility, how many deliverable versions, and whether post is done in the incentive jurisdiction.
- **Engine.** Post calendar with dependencies: picture lock gates the mix, the mix gates the deliverables, VFX finals gate the DI. A slipped lock pushes facility bookings that are already paid for. Deliverables list as a checklist with per-item cost and a delivery gate that releases money.
- **Takes.** Feature post typically 20-40 weeks from wrap to delivery
- **Television.** Post runs in a rolling pipeline with several episodes at different stages at once, against a hard airdate. There is no slipping lock: the show airs. Episodic post is staffed for throughput, not for one object.

### Visual effects budgeting and bidding

Shots are counted, categorised by complexity, and bid to vendors per shot or per package. Awards are split across vendors, with an allowance for shot creep and versioning.

- **Who.** VFX Producer and VFX Supervisor
- **Decides.** Shot count, complexity tier, in-camera versus post, vendor split, how many iterations are included, and how much creep allowance to carry.
- **Engine.** Shot objects with complexity, vendor, bid, iterations included, actual iterations and status. Shot count creep is the norm, so a VFX budget with a fixed shot count is a lie: model the creep rate and the per-iteration overage.
- **Takes.** Bidding 4-8 weeks; execution runs 6-18 months
- **Television.** Per-episode shot allowance against a season package deal. Episodes blow the allowance unevenly, and the season is managed by trading allowance between episodes.

### Music budget

Composer fee or package, orchestra and scoring stage, licensed masters and sync, clearances by territory and term, music editor, and re-use/new-use fees for musicians.

- **Who.** Music Supervisor and Composer, with Business Affairs
- **Decides.** Package deal versus separate line items, score versus source, which songs to clear, what term and territory to buy, and whether to accept a festival-only licence now and top up later.
- **Engine.** Cue sheet with per-cue rights records: master owner, publisher, fee, term, territory, media. A festival-only licence that is never upgraded blocks delivery. Rights expiry creates a future liability on a picture that is still being licensed.
- **Takes.** Clearance 6-16 weeks; scoring 4-10 weeks
- **Television.** Per-episode music budget against a season-wide composer package; needle-drops are per-episode and the show's music budget is a running total that gets rationed.

### Insurance package placement

A producers package (cast, negative/faulty stock, props sets and wardrobe, extra expense, miscellaneous equipment, third-party property damage), plus general liability, auto, workers' comp, umbrella and Errors & Omissions.

- **Who.** Producer with an entertainment insurance broker
- **Decides.** Limits and deductibles, which cast are scheduled, whether to buy weather cover, and whether to accept exclusions the bond company will then refuse to accept.
- **Engine.** Policy objects with peril, limit, deductible, exclusions and named-insured list. A claim must actually pay: a cast illness that costs 3 shoot days recovers the insured portion above the deductible and the uninsured remainder becomes an overage. Exclusions must be able to bite.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks to place; E&O often later, before delivery
- **Television.** Annual or per-season policies with per-episode reporting, and cast cover that must survive a multi-year series commitment.

### Contingency setting

A percentage of the budget is carried as contingency — conventionally 10% for an independent feature, sometimes 5% on a studio picture with no bond, higher for period, water, animals, children or foreign shoots.

- **Who.** Producer, negotiated with the financiers and the completion guarantor
- **Decides.** The contingency percentage, and therefore how much room the picture has before the bond is exposed and the guarantor's rights activate.
- **Engine.** Contingency is a real account balance that can be drawn against, with an approval gate on each draw and a running remaining balance. It must be the trigger condition for bond powers, not decoration. A sweep across contingency levels must change how often the guarantor takes over.
- **Takes.** Set at greenlight; drawn against throughout the shoot
- **Television.** Series carry a smaller contingency per episode (often 3-5%) plus a season-level reserve, because the season can self-correct across episodes in a way a single feature cannot.

### Completion bond application

The bond package goes in: budget, schedule, script, cash flow, key crew CVs, cast and director deals, insurance, chain of title, financing plan and the interparty structure.

- **Who.** Producer and Line Producer, submitting to the guarantor
- **Decides.** Which guarantor to approach, and how much of the picture's weakness to disclose up front versus have found.
- **Engine.** An application object scored by the guarantor against the schedule's realism (pages per day versus the director's history), crew experience, cast risk, location risk, VFX exposure and the producer's track record. The score sets the fee, the exclusions and whether a bond is offered at all.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks from submission to a conditional offer
- **Television.** Studio and network television is generally unbonded — the studio self-guarantees. Bonds appear on independent and international co-production television, where the financiers demand one.

### Guarantor's budget and schedule review

The guarantor's production people re-break the script, re-schedule it and re-budget it, and come back with a list of what is underbudgeted and underscheduled. This is the real negotiation.

- **Who.** The completion guarantor's own line producer / production executive
- **Decides.** Whether the picture is bondable as presented, and what must be added — days, contingency, crew, a stronger UPM — before it is.
- **Engine.** A shadow budget and shadow schedule computed from the same script by a stricter model, producing a delta list per account. The player must be able to argue lines back, accept some, and pay for the rest.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks
- **Television.** On bonded television the review is of the pattern budget and one representative episode, then the amortization and above-pattern assumptions across the whole order.

### Bond negotiation: fee, strike price and exclusions

The guarantee is priced. Fee is typically quoted at 2-3% of the strike price with a no-claims rebate often of 25-50% of the fee. Exclusions are negotiated: named perils, cast, weather, force majeure, and sometimes VFX.

- **Who.** Producer, guarantor and financiers' counsel
- **Decides.** Fee level, rebate share, what the strike price excludes, which exclusions to accept, and whether the fee is cash or deferred into the waterfall.
- **Engine.** Strike price = budget less contingency, bond fee, financing costs, publicity and named exclusions. The guarantor's obligation attaches only above the strike price, i.e. after the producer's contingency is gone. Model both numbers separately or the bond is a word.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks of negotiation
- **Television.** Bonded series price per season with a cross-episode obligation, so a cheap episode can fund an expensive one before the guarantee is touched.

### Interparty agreement and security

Bank, gap lender, tax-credit lender, guarantor, sales agent and equity all sign one document setting priority, cure rights, notice, step-in and who may enforce against the collateral.

- **Who.** Production counsel for each financier
- **Decides.** Recoupment order, who controls the picture on default, whose consent is needed to change the budget, and who gets the delivery materials.
- **Engine.** A priority-ordered list of parties with a rights matrix: consent rights, notice rights, step-in rights, enforcement rights. It must be readable by the waterfall and by the takeover process, or none of it is real.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks; often the long pole in closing
- **Television.** Simpler where a single studio or platform finances, replaced by a production services agreement. Complex on international co-productions with two national funds.

### Chain of title and copyright security

Every link from author to production company is documented — option, purchase, writer agreements, releases, clearances — then the copyright is mortgaged to the lender and a UCC-1 filed.

- **Who.** Production counsel
- **Decides.** Whether the rights are clean enough to bond, insure and deliver, and what to do about a gap in the chain.
- **Engine.** A rights chain as linked records with signature status and gaps. A gap must block E&O, which must block delivery, which must block the delivery payment. For the father-as-screenwriter this is where his own paper enters the picture's balance sheet.
- **Takes.** Weeks to months; a single missing release can stall for a year
- **Television.** Underlying rights get optioned for series with exclusive periods, sequel and spin-off rights, and per-season payments that continue as long as the show runs.

### Finance plan and sources-and-uses

A one-page statement of where every dollar comes from (equity, pre-sales, gap, tax credit, soft money, deferrals) and where it goes (negative cost, financing costs, fees, bond, contingency).

- **Who.** Producer
- **Decides.** The capital stack: how much equity to give up, how much debt to carry, how many territories to pre-sell versus hold, and which incentive jurisdiction to shoot in.
- **Engine.** Sources must equal uses to the cent, with each source carrying its own cost of money, drawdown timing, condition precedent and collateral. Change the shoot jurisdiction and the incentive changes, which changes the gap, which changes the interest, which changes the budget.
- **Takes.** Iterated over months; refinalised at closing
- **Television.** Television plans around a licence fee that arrives on a delivery schedule; the deficit is the amount to be financed, and the plan is really a plan to carry the deficit until international sales close it.

### Equity raise and investor documentation

An SPV is formed, a private placement memorandum or subscription documents drafted, investors solicited within the relevant securities exemption, and capital called into escrow.

- **Who.** Producer with securities counsel
- **Decides.** Entity type, minimum raise, investor recoupment premium (commonly 110-120% before profit split), profit split, control rights and whether to give a co-producer credit.
- **Engine.** Investor records with commitment, funded amount, recoupment premium, waterfall position and consent rights. The premium is a real multiplier in the waterfall; change it from 110% to 120% and the point at which profits start must move.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months, frequently the longest single process
- **Television.** Rarer — equity in television goes to a deficit-financing studio or an international distributor, not to individual investors, and is recouped from licence fees and distribution rather than box office.

### Pre-sales and minimum guarantees

Distribution rights are licensed territory by territory and media by media for a minimum guarantee, evidenced by a long-form or a deal memo with a delivery-conditional payment.

- **Who.** Sales Agent, at a market or ahead of one
- **Decides.** Which territories to sell now versus hold for a better cast, what price to accept, term and media, and whether to take a smaller MG from a stronger buyer whose paper a bank will discount.
- **Engine.** Territory records with market value bands that respond to cast, genre, director and budget; buyer records with creditworthiness that determines whether a bank will lend against their contract. An unbankable buyer's MG is worth nothing at closing.
- **Takes.** Markets run on a calendar — AFM, EFM/Berlin, Cannes, TIFF, and Mip/LA Screenings for television
- **Television.** Sold as a finished series or a format, often in packages and output deals, at the LA Screenings and MIPCOM rather than the film markets. Second and third windows matter more than the first.

### Discounting pre-sale contracts at a bank

Executed pre-sale contracts are assigned to a lender who advances cash now against the contracted payment later, discounted for time, currency and buyer credit risk.

- **Who.** Producer and the bank's entertainment lending group
- **Decides.** Which contracts to discount, at what haircut, and whether to accept a bank's demand for a completion bond and an interparty as a condition.
- **Engine.** Each contract has face value, payment trigger (usually delivery), buyer credit rating and currency. The advance is face less interest, less fees, less a credit haircut. Interest accrues on the drawn balance from draw date to repayment — a real cost line, not a footnote.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks to close a facility
- **Television.** Banks lend against a network or platform licence agreement, which is investment-grade paper and discounts far more cheaply than a foreign pre-sale.

### Gap and super-gap financing

A loan advanced against the estimated value of UNSOLD territories, secured by the picture and typically limited to a fraction of unsold estimates, at a much higher rate than senior debt.

- **Who.** Producer with a specialist lender
- **Decides.** Whether to take gap at all, how much, and what coverage ratio the lender demands (commonly 200% of the loan in unsold estimates).
- **Engine.** A sales estimate table per territory with high/low/ask, from which the lender computes coverage. Interest rate scales with the coverage ratio and the cast. Gap that is never repaid from sales must actually default and take the picture's collateral.
- **Takes.** 6-10 weeks
- **Television.** Less common; the equivalent is borrowing against an international distribution advance for a series with no committed buyer outside the home market.

### Tax credit and incentive application

An application is filed with the jurisdiction before principal photography, a reservation or allocation letter issued, and the production then tracks qualified spend separately from the start.

- **Who.** Production Accountant with an incentive consultant, filed by the producer
- **Decides.** Which jurisdiction to shoot in, how much local hire to use, whether to chase an uplift (out-of-zone, local hire, VFX), and what is coded as qualifying.
- **Engine.** Per-jurisdiction rules as data: base rate, uplifts and their conditions, qualifying-cost definitions, ATL caps, per-project caps, minimum spend, application window, audit requirement and payout lag. California 4.0 is a worked example — refundable, non-transferable except for independents, $750M/year, $120M qualified-spend cap on features.
- **Takes.** Application weeks before shoot; certification 3-18 months after delivery
- **Television.** Applied for per season, with renewal risk between seasons. A show that loses its allocation in year three has a cost problem it cannot fix creatively.

### Incentive monetisation

The credit is turned into cash before it arrives — borrowed against at a discount, or, where transferable, sold to a local taxpayer at a discount to face.

- **Who.** Producer with a tax credit lender or broker
- **Decides.** Loan versus sale, which lender or broker, and what discount to accept. Transferable credits typically trade at a discount to face; refundable credits are borrowed against.
- **Engine.** Credit object with estimated value, jurisdiction, transferability, expected certification date and payout lag. The financing cost is a function of that lag — an 18-month lag at a real interest rate is a budget line in the hundreds of thousands on a mid-size picture.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks to place; repaid on certification
- **Television.** Credits are earned per season and can be monetised on a rolling basis, which stabilises a series' cash flow in a way a one-off feature never gets.

### Soft money and co-production structuring

Treaty co-productions, regional funds, national film funds, sale-leasebacks and rebates are stacked, each with its own spend, nationality and cultural-test conditions.

- **Who.** Producer with international counsel and a co-producer
- **Decides.** Which country to make the picture in, which co-producer to partner with, and how much creative control and ownership to trade for the money.
- **Engine.** Each scheme carries eligibility conditions on nationality of key crew, spend location, cultural points and minimum co-producer share. Conditions must be checkable against the actual crew list and the actual spend, and failing one must actually forfeit the money.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months; treaty approval is slow
- **Television.** Television co-productions split territories and sometimes shoot different versions; broadcaster co-pros bring editorial conditions that change the script, not just the budget.

### Deferrals and deferments

Fees are agreed but not paid now; they sit in the waterfall at a stated position and are paid only if the picture recoups to that point.

- **Who.** Producer, negotiating with cast, crew, vendors and facilities
- **Decides.** Who defers, how much, whether the deferral sits ahead of or behind equity, and whether it converts to points instead.
- **Engine.** Deferment records with amount, waterfall position and a paid/unpaid state. Deferments ahead of equity poison the raise; deferments behind it may never pay. A sweep across deferral position must change both the ability to close financing and the eventual payout.
- **Takes.** Negotiated during closing; settled years later or never
- **Television.** Rare in studio television, common in independent and first-season shows where a showrunner or cast defer against a back-nine pickup.

### Cash flow schedule construction

The budget is spread week by week — sometimes day by day — across prep, shoot, wrap and post, showing what must be in the bank each Friday.

- **Who.** Production Accountant with the Line Producer
- **Decides.** Draw timing, what to pay late, whether to front-load a deposit to lock a vendor, and how much float to hold.
- **Engine.** Weekly cost curve per account, summed to a weekly cash requirement, matched against source drawdown dates. A negative balance in any week is a real failure state — payroll that cannot be met stops the picture even if the budget is fine. Cash flow failure must be able to kill a picture that is on budget.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days to build; reforecast weekly
- **Television.** A season's cash flow has a sawtooth from episodic delivery payments, and the deficit peaks mid-season. The financier's exposure is a curve, not a number.

### Financial close

Every condition precedent is satisfied at once, documents are signed, the collection account opened, security perfected, and the first draw released.

- **Who.** Production counsel, all financiers, the guarantor and the bank
- **Decides.** Nothing, by design — it is the point at which all earlier decisions either hold or fail. Missing one condition delays everyone.
- **Engine.** A conditions-precedent checklist that must be wholly satisfied before money moves. Any unsatisfied item blocks the close and pushes the start date, which pushes the schedule, which may cost a cast member their availability.
- **Takes.** A single day after 6-16 weeks of getting there
- **Television.** Usually a production services agreement and a purchase order from the studio rather than a syndicated close; days, not months.

### Collection Account Management Agreement

A neutral third party opens a bank account into which all receipts flow, and distributes them strictly per the agreed recoupment schedule, reporting to every signatory.

- **Who.** Producer, the CAM (Fintage House, Freeway Entertainment), and every party in the waterfall
- **Decides.** Which CAM, the fee (a percentage of receipts with a minimum and a cap), who countersigns, and the reporting cadence.
- **Engine.** The collection account is a real ledger: receipts in, waterfall applied, payments out, statements generated. Every signatory sees the same numbers. Without it, the sales agent's own accounting is the only record, which is exactly the failure the CAM exists to prevent.
- **Takes.** 2-4 weeks to paper; runs for the life of the picture
- **Television.** Used on independent and co-produced series; a fully studio-financed show has no CAM because the studio is the collection point and the only counterparty.

### Waterfall drafting

The recoupment schedule is written: the exact order in which every dollar of receipts is applied, from the CAM's own fee down to net profits.

- **Who.** Production counsel, agreed by all financiers
- **Decides.** Order, caps, corridors, cross-collateralisation, and whether any party gets a first-dollar corridor ahead of senior debt.
- **Engine.** An ordered list of positions, each with a claim amount, a cap, a percentage and a state. Applying receipts must walk the list and stop where the money runs out. Move one position up and the payouts of everyone below it must change.
- **Takes.** Negotiated over weeks during closing
- **Television.** Television's equivalent is the Modified Adjusted Gross Receipts definition and distribution fee structure, where the fees and the definition of receipts matter more than the order.

### Purchase order and commitment control

No money is spent without a numbered PO coded to an account. The PO creates a COMMITMENT the moment it is issued, long before an invoice arrives.

- **Who.** Production Accountant and the department heads
- **Decides.** Approval thresholds, who can issue a PO, and whether to approve one that pushes an account over.
- **Engine.** Three balances per account: actual (invoiced and paid), committed (PO issued, not invoiced) and estimated to complete. Cost = actual + committed + ETC. A system that only tracks invoices reports a picture on budget right up to the week the invoices land.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** POs carry an episode code as well as an account, and a PO that spans episodes must be split at issue.

### Petty cash, floats and purchasing cards

Cash advances and cards are issued to department heads, reconciled against receipts with account coding, and reissued. Uncoded or unreceipted spend is charged back to the holder.

- **Who.** Assistant Accountant and the Key Accountant
- **Decides.** Float size per department, card limits, and how hard to be about unreceipted spend.
- **Engine.** Float records with issued amount, spent amount, receipts reconciled and an ageing. Unreconciled floats are a real risk: they hide cost until wrap and then appear as a lump. Incentive auditors disallow unreceipted cash.
- **Takes.** Continuous; reconciled weekly
- **Television.** Season-long floats that roll rather than closing out, so the ageing problem compounds.

### Timecards, start work and payroll processing

Start paperwork is collected, daily timecards captured from the 2nd AD's production report, coded, approved, transmitted to the payroll house, and returned as a payroll register with fringes computed.

- **Who.** Payroll Accountant with the payroll service (EP, Cast & Crew, GreenSlate, Wrapbook)
- **Decides.** Payroll service and its handling fee, pay cycle, who approves timecards, and whether to challenge a claimed penalty.
- **Engine.** Timecard objects with in/out, meals, travel, penalties claimed and account code. The payroll register must be computed from the guild agreement, not entered. Payroll and its fringes are typically the majority of below-the-line spend — get this wrong and nothing downstream is right.
- **Takes.** Weekly cycle; register back in 2-4 business days
- **Television.** Runs for the whole season with the same crew, so ceilings, vacation/holiday accruals and annual fringe caps all bite mid-season.

### Hot costs

A one-page next-morning report of yesterday's variable costs: crew overtime hours, meal penalties, forced calls, background headcount, raw media, catering counts and box rentals, against the day's allowance.

- **Who.** Production Accountant, from the 2nd AD's Daily Production Report
- **Decides.** Whether to change today's call, cut a scene, add a meal break, or send a unit home early — decisions made on the day, on this number.
- **Engine.** Per-day actual versus per-day allowance, by category, delivered before the next call time. This is the fastest feedback loop in production and the only one that lets a player intervene before the money is spent.
- **Takes.** Delivered by 10am the following day
- **Television.** Hot costs carry an episode code, and the running per-episode total against pattern is the number the showrunner is shown.

### Weekly cost report

Account by account: original budget, approved revisions, cost to date, commitments, cost to complete, estimated final cost, and variance. Signed by the producer and issued to every financier.

- **Who.** Production Accountant, reviewed by the UPM, Line Producer, studio and guarantor
- **Decides.** What the picture now believes it will cost, which is the number that triggers everything else — bond involvement, studio intervention, contingency draws.
- **Engine.** EFC = actual + committed + cost to complete, per account, rolled to the top sheet. Cost to complete is a forecast that must be re-estimated, not a subtraction. Variance is the headline; the TREND across weeks is the real signal and must be stored as a series, not a snapshot.
- **Takes.** Weekly during shoot, issued 5-8 days after week end; bi-weekly in prep, monthly in post
- **Television.** Both a per-episode cost report and a season-to-date report, plus an amortization report. An episode can be over while the season is on pattern, and only the season number matters to the studio.

### Trend and flash reporting

Between formal cost reports, a short forward-looking estimate of where the picture is heading, based on days behind, overtime run-rate and known upcoming exposures.

- **Who.** Line Producer and Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Whether to act now or wait for the cost report — and acting early is the only cheap option available.
- **Engine.** A projection from current run-rate: days behind x cost per day + overtime trend + known exposures. It must be able to disagree with the cost report and be right, because the cost report lags reality by a week.
- **Takes.** Produced in an hour; issued mid-week
- **Television.** Projected across the remaining episode order, so a single bad episode shows up as a season-end number the studio can see coming.

### Schedule variance tracking (days behind)

Scenes and pages completed against scenes and pages scheduled, expressed as fractions of a day ahead or behind, tracked cumulatively from day one.

- **Who.** First Assistant Director and UPM
- **Decides.** Whether to add a day, work a sixth day, split the unit, drop scenes, or ask for more money — and the earlier the cheaper.
- **Engine.** Per-day scheduled versus completed in pages and setups; cumulative days behind. Days behind x fully-loaded cost per day is the single best predictor of final overage, and must be wired to the cost forecast. Half a day behind on day 5 is a different object from half a day behind on day 40.
- **Takes.** Updated every night with the production report
- **Television.** Measured against the pattern's days-per-episode. Falling behind on episode 3 is repaid by a cheaper episode 4 or it becomes a season overage.

### Contingency draw

Money is moved from the contingency account into a specific production account to cover a specific, identified overage, with a written justification.

- **Who.** Producer requests; studio, financiers or guarantor approve
- **Decides.** When to draw, how much, and which account — and whether to draw at all rather than absorb it elsewhere.
- **Engine.** Each draw is a posted transaction reducing a real contingency balance with an approval record and a reason. Contingency remaining is the countdown to the guarantor's rights activating. A picture that draws 60% of contingency by day 10 should be in a visibly different state.
- **Takes.** Hours to days for approval, depending on the approver
- **Television.** Drawn against the season reserve, often to cover an above-pattern episode that everyone knew was coming.

### Budget revision and re-forecast

A formally approved change to the budget — added days, a rate change, a script change — producing a revised budget against which variance is thereafter measured.

- **Who.** Line Producer with the Production Accountant
- **Decides.** Whether to revise the budget (making the overage official and the baseline honest) or hold the original and carry the variance (keeping pressure on).
- **Engine.** Versioned budgets with an approval chain, so variance can be reported against original AND against current. A producer who keeps revising to zero variance is hiding an overage, and the system should let the studio see both numbers.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per revision
- **Television.** Pattern revisions apply forward to all remaining episodes, so one revision multiplies by the remaining order.

### Savings sweep and reallocation

An account that comes in under is either released back to the picture for use elsewhere, or swept by the studio and removed from the production's reach.

- **Who.** Studio Finance or the Line Producer, depending on who has the right
- **Decides.** Whether to declare a saving at all, or sit on it against a future exposure — which is exactly why studios sweep.
- **Engine.** A savings pool with a policy flag: producer-controlled, studio-swept, or shared. Change the policy and the producer's optimal behaviour changes — under a sweep regime, savings are hidden, which is a real and modellable behaviour.
- **Takes.** At each cost report
- **Television.** Savings are traded between episodes within the season much more freely than between accounts on a feature.

### Overage notification and escalation

Once the estimated final cost exceeds the budget by the threshold in the financing documents, formal notice must be given — usually with a plan attached.

- **Who.** Line Producer to the studio / financiers / guarantor
- **Decides.** When to tell, how much to tell, and whether to present a cure alongside the problem. Late notice destroys more credibility than the overage itself.
- **Engine.** A notice obligation triggered by a threshold in the finance documents, with a deadline. Failing to notify must be a breach with consequences — the guarantor's takeover rights, a default under the loan — not a wrist-slap.
- **Takes.** Immediate; the contractual notice period is typically days
- **Television.** Networks and streamers expect the notice against the season, and the conversation is about the remaining order rather than about a single unit.

### Cutting to fit

Scenes are cut, combined, moved to a cheaper location, or rewritten to remove an element, until the remaining schedule fits the remaining money.

- **Who.** Producer and Director, with the writer — and here the father-as-screenwriter is the person who can do it well
- **Decides.** What the picture loses. This is the single most consequential creative decision the money makes, and it is made under time pressure with the crew already hired.
- **Engine.** A cut must propagate: removing a scene removes its elements, its day, its cast day, the location, the fringes, and changes the picture's quality inputs. If a cut only reduces a number and never affects the finished film, the whole system is decorative.
- **Takes.** Hours to days, usually overnight
- **Television.** Rewriting a future episode to be cheap is easier than cutting the one in front of you, so television absorbs overages forward. A 'bottle episode' is exactly this process made routine.

### Guarantor monitoring during production

The bond rep receives cost reports, daily production reports and call sheets, watches dailies, visits the set, and attends the weekly production meeting.

- **Who.** The completion guarantor's representative
- **Decides.** Whether to warn, to impose conditions, to require a cure plan, or to take over. Their leverage rises as contingency falls.
- **Engine.** A guarantor agent with a state machine: monitoring -> concerned -> formal notice -> cure period -> takeover. Transitions driven by days behind, contingency remaining and EFC trend. It must be possible to talk the guarantor down with a credible plan, and possible to fail.
- **Takes.** Continuous through prep, shoot and post until delivery
- **Television.** On bonded series, monitoring is at season level with per-episode reporting, and the cure is usually redistributing the remaining order.

### Bond takeover

The guarantor exercises its contractual right to take over the production: it can replace the director or the producer, change the schedule, cut the script and control the money, and it funds completion.

- **Who.** The completion guarantor, displacing the producer
- **Decides.** Nothing for the player, which is the point — the player has lost control of their own picture. Their only remaining decisions are whether to cooperate and what they can salvage.
- **Engine.** A takeover must actually transfer control: the player loses approval over schedule, cast changes and spending, the guarantor's cheaper choices are applied, and the finished picture is measurably different. A takeover that only changes the prose is the exact failure this project exists to prevent.
- **Takes.** Immediate on notice; runs to delivery
- **Television.** Almost never happens in studio television; on an independent series the guarantor takes over the remaining episodes.

### Abandonment election

Instead of completing, the guarantor may elect to abandon the picture and repay the financiers their advanced funds, taking the assets.

- **Who.** The completion guarantor
- **Decides.** Whether completing is cheaper than repaying. If the remaining cost to complete exceeds what the financiers put in, abandonment is the rational choice and the picture is never finished.
- **Engine.** A comparison of cost-to-complete against total financier advances, run at the moment of takeover. The film ceases to exist, the financiers are made whole, everyone attached loses their credit and their backend, and the producer's reputation takes a permanent hit.
- **Takes.** The election is made in days
- **Television.** Effectively unavailable mid-season for a show with an airdate and committed advertising; the network absorbs it instead.

### No-claims rebate

If the guarantor never advanced funds, an agreed share of the bond fee — commonly 25-50% — is returned to the production or to the financiers.

- **Who.** Producer claims from the guarantor at delivery
- **Decides.** Where the rebate goes: to the production's bottom line, to the waterfall, or to the producer.
- **Engine.** A conditional credit that pays only if advanced-funds equals zero. Tiny in money, useful as a design object: it gives the producer a cash reason to stay inside contingency, which is a real behavioural pressure.
- **Takes.** Paid weeks after delivery
- **Television.** Same mechanic where a bond exists at all.

### Final cost report and wrap audit

Every account closed, every commitment cleared or released, accruals booked, and a final cost statement issued. Books are then audited for the financiers and the guarantor.

- **Who.** Production Accountant, then an independent auditor
- **Decides.** What the picture actually cost — the negative cost that every participation, every recoupment and every incentive claim is computed from.
- **Engine.** A closed ledger with accruals, released commitments and a reconciled cash position. The final negative cost becomes the input to the waterfall, the participation definitions and the incentive claim. It must be one number that three systems read.
- **Takes.** 8-16 weeks after wrap; audit 4-12 weeks after that
- **Television.** Per-episode final costs plus a season final, with amortization allocated. The per-episode number is what gets compared to the licence fee.

### Incentive audit and certification

Qualified spend is examined line by line against the jurisdiction's rules, disallowances made, and a certification issued stating the credit amount earned.

- **Who.** An approved CPA or the jurisdiction's own auditors, engaged by the producer
- **Decides.** How aggressively to claim, whether to contest a disallowance, and whether the audit cost (itself usually non-qualifying) is worth a marginal claim.
- **Engine.** A qualifying test per transaction: was the spend in-jurisdiction, was the vendor local, was the labour resident, is the category allowed, is it under the ATL cap. Disallowance rate is the gap between estimated and certified credit — and that gap is a real financing hole that must be funded from somewhere.
- **Takes.** 3-9 months after delivery; payment 1-12 months after certification
- **Television.** Audited per season, with the risk that a rule change between seasons reduces the credit a returning show was budgeted against.

### Delivery and the delivery schedule

Every item on the distributor's delivery schedule is produced and accepted: masters, textless, M&E, dubbing scripts, chain of title, E&O certificate, music cue sheets, credits, QC reports, key art elements.

- **Who.** Post Production Supervisor and the producer's delivery coordinator
- **Decides.** What to spend on deliverables and when — and whether to accept a distributor's technical spec that costs more than the item is worth.
- **Engine.** A delivery checklist where each item gates payment. An unaccepted item blocks the MG, which blocks the bank repayment, which accrues interest. Delivery failure is a cash event, not an administrative one, and is a common way a finished picture still loses money.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks after picture lock; acceptance can take months
- **Television.** Delivery is per episode on a rolling schedule against airdates, and a late episode is a broadcast crisis rather than a payment delay.

### Residuals reserve and guild reporting

Ongoing payments to SAG-AFTRA, WGA and DGA members for reuse — broadcast, streaming, home video, foreign — computed under each agreement and reported on a schedule.

- **Who.** Studio or distributor's residuals department; the producer on an independent
- **Decides.** Whether to reserve for residuals in the finance plan, and who assumes the liability on an assignment of distribution rights.
- **Engine.** A residual liability that accrues from exploitation events by media and territory, paid forward for decades. It survives delivery, follows the picture through assignments, and must be a standing liability on the picture's balance sheet — the clearest case of a decision with a 30-year tail.
- **Takes.** Reported quarterly; liability runs for the life of the copyright
- **Television.** Far larger in television because of repeat exhibition and streaming reuse, and it is a standard reason an old show is expensive to keep on a service.

### Waterfall application and participation statements

Receipts arrive, the recoupment schedule is applied in order, statements are issued to every participant showing what came in, what was deducted and what position the picture has reached.

- **Who.** The Collection Account Manager, or the studio's participations department
- **Decides.** Nothing at this stage by design — but whether to audit the statement is a real decision, and audits routinely find money.
- **Engine.** Walk the ordered waterfall against actual receipts; each position's claim reduces until satisfied or the money runs out. Statements must be generatable for any participant at any date, and must be capable of being WRONG in ways an audit can find — which is the honest model of this business.
- **Takes.** Statements quarterly for the first years, then semi-annually and annually
- **Television.** Modified Adjusted Gross Receipts with a distribution fee and a distribution expense deduction; on a fully-owned streaming show there may be no receipts at all, only a cost-plus fee already paid.

### Television pattern budget and amortization

A pattern budget is set for the typical episode; shared costs — pilot, standing sets, showrunner, main titles, series regular holding — are amortized across the order; episodes that exceed pattern are flagged above-pattern.

- **Who.** Line Producer with the studio's television finance executive
- **Decides.** The pattern number, the amortization method (straight-line, weighted, front-loaded), and which costs are pattern versus amortized versus above-pattern.
- **Engine.** Three cost pools: per-episode, amortized (with a method and a denominator = episode count), and above-pattern. Change the episode order from 10 to 13 and the per-episode amortized charge must fall. Cost per episode is meaningless without the amortization method attached.
- **Takes.** Built once per season; revised at any order change
- **Television.** This process has no feature equivalent at all. It is the structural difference: a feature has one budget, a season has a pattern plus a spread plus exceptions.

### Licence fee negotiation and deficit calculation

The buyer pays a licence fee per episode for defined rights and a defined term; the difference between that fee and the cost of production is the deficit the studio carries.

- **Who.** Studio Business Affairs against the network or platform
- **Decides.** Fee level, term, territory, holdbacks, and how much deficit the studio will absorb against future international and library value.
- **Engine.** Licence fee versus per-episode cost, producing a per-season deficit and a cumulative deficit across seasons. Recouped from international sales, syndication and library — a model that must run for years and can end with the show never recouping. This is the central financial object of television.
- **Takes.** Negotiated before the season is ordered
- **Television.** Entirely a television process. The streaming alternative is cost-plus: the platform funds the full budget plus a markup (commonly 10-30%, estimate) and takes all rights, so there is no deficit and no backend — a different game with different optimal play.

### Studio overhead, producer fee and charge-backs

The studio charges the picture an overhead percentage, a facility charge for stages and services, and books the producer fee. On the lot side these are revenue.

- **Who.** Studio Finance and the lot's operations management
- **Decides.** Overhead rate, what the lot charges its own productions versus third parties, and whether to discount for occupancy — a decision the father-as-lot-owner makes on both sides of the ledger.
- **Engine.** Charge-backs must post to the picture's accounts AND to the lot's revenue in the same double-entry ledger. A discount to fill a stage reduces picture cost and lot revenue by the same amount. Model the lot's occupancy, fixed cost and break-even, or a discount is free money.
- **Takes.** Set at greenlight; billed monthly
- **Television.** A returning series is the lot's base load and gets a term rate; features pay a premium for short flexible bookings.

### Currency exposure and hedging

A picture budgeted in one currency and financed or spent in another is hedged with forwards, or left open and exposed.

- **Who.** Producer with the bank's treasury desk
- **Decides.** Whether to hedge, how much of the exposure, and at what forward rate — and whether the cost of the hedge is worth the certainty.
- **Engine.** A budget with per-account currency, a spot rate that moves over the production calendar, and hedge contracts with a rate and a notional. An unhedged foreign shoot must be able to go over purely on exchange rate, which has really happened to real pictures.
- **Takes.** Placed at closing; settles on payment dates
- **Television.** Season-long exposure across multiple years for a show shooting abroad; hedges are rolled and a multi-year move can make a location uneconomic between seasons.

### Insurance claim and cover shooting

When a cast member is ill, a location is lost or a camera negative fails, days are documented, the claim filed, and cover sets or cover scenes shot to keep the unit working.

- **Who.** UPM and the Production Accountant, with the broker and loss adjuster
- **Decides.** Whether to claim (the deductible and the premium consequence may exceed the loss), what to shoot instead today, and whether to shut down.
- **Engine.** A claim object with covered loss, deductible, adjuster's disallowance and settlement lag. The uninsured remainder is an overage that hits contingency. Cover sets must be a real standing resource the schedule can fall back on — having one is the difference between a lost day and a costed one.
- **Takes.** Claim filed in days; settled in 1-6 months
- **Television.** A series carries standing cover sets on stage by design, which is why episodic television absorbs weather loss so much better than a location feature.

### Prep, shoot and wrap payroll close-out

Final timecards, vacation and holiday pay, unused box rental, kit fee true-ups, crew deal memo reconciliations, and the last payroll are processed and the employer accounts closed.

- **Who.** Production Accountant and the payroll service
- **Decides.** What contested penalties to pay, and how to settle disputed hours rather than have them arrive as a guild claim months later.
- **Engine.** An open-items list that ages. Unsettled penalty claims become guild grievances with interest, arriving after the final cost report is issued — a real mechanism for a picture's cost to rise after it is finished.
- **Takes.** 4-10 weeks after wrap
- **Television.** Rolls into hiatus rather than closing, and crew retention over hiatus is itself a budgeted cost.

### Option spaces

**Financing sources in the plan** — 26 options

`Single-investor equity` · `Equity fund / slate fund` · `Strategic or brand equity` · `Crowdfunded equity` · `Producer's own cash` · `Territory pre-sale with minimum guarantee` · `Output deal with a distributor` · `Negative pickup guarantee from a studio` · `Distribution advance` · `Network or platform licence fee` · `Streamer cost-plus commission` · `Full studio finance` · `Senior bank loan discounting contracts` · `Gap loan against unsold estimates` · `Super-gap / mezzanine` · `Tax credit loan` · `Sale of a transferable tax credit` · `Cash rebate or grant` · `Treaty co-production funding` · `National or regional film fund` · `Sale-leaseback` · `Deferrals (cast, crew, producer)` · `Facility, lab or post deferral` · `Product placement and brand integration` · `Bridge loan against the bond` · `Library or residual collateral`

**Completion guarantor** — 6 options

`Film Finances Inc.` · `UniFi Completion Guaranty Insurance Solutions` · `Media Guarantors` · `A studio self-guarantee` · `No bond, financier waiver` · `No bond because the studio fully finances`

**Contingency level** — 8 options

`0%` · `2.5%` · `5%` · `7.5%` · `10%` · `12.5%` · `15%` · `20%`

**Bond fee and rebate structure** — 9 options

`Percentage of strike price` · `Percentage of total budget` · `Minimum flat fee for small pictures` · `Fee with 50% no-claims rebate` · `Fee with 25% no-claims rebate` · `Fee with no rebate` · `Fee plus separate premium for a named exclusion (weather, a specific cast member, water, animals)` · `Fee deferred into the waterfall` · `Fee split between production and financiers`

**Chart of accounts template** — 7 options

`Studio chart (per-studio, e.g. a major's own numbering)` · `Movie Magic / EP Budgeting default` · `Showbiz Budgeting default` · `AICP (commercials only)` · `Broadcaster template (BBC, ITV, a streamer's own)` · `Financier- or bond-mandated chart` · `Custom chart`

**Union footing** — 18 options

`Full signatory: SAG-AFTRA, DGA, WGA, IATSE, Teamsters` · `SAG-AFTRA Basic/Theatrical only` · `SAG-AFTRA Low Budget Agreement` · `SAG-AFTRA Modified Low Budget` · `SAG-AFTRA Ultra Low Budget` · `SAG-AFTRA Short Project` · `SAG-AFTRA New Media` · `IATSE Basic Agreement` · `IATSE Area Standards Agreement` · `IATSE Low Budget Theatrical (tiered)` · `Teamsters Local 399 / Basic Crafts` · `DGA Basic Agreement` · `DGA low-budget sideletter` · `WGA MBA` · `WGA low-budget agreement` · `Non-union` · `Mixed / hybrid footing` · `Foreign local agreement (BECTU/Pact, ACTRA, MEAA)`

**Incentive jurisdiction** — 34 options

`California (Program 4.0)` · `New York` · `Georgia` · `New Mexico` · `Louisiana` · `Illinois` · `New Jersey` · `Massachusetts` · `Texas` · `Oklahoma` · `Kentucky` · `Utah` · `Nevada` · `Hawaii` · `United Kingdom (AVEC / IFTC)` · `Ireland (Section 481)` · `Canada — British Columbia` · `Canada — Ontario` · `Canada — Quebec` · `Australia` · `New Zealand` · `Hungary` · `Czech Republic` · `Spain (mainland and Canary Islands)` · `Italy` · `Malta` · `Iceland` · `South Africa` · `Morocco` · `Jordan` · `Thailand` · `Colombia` · `Dominican Republic` · `No incentive`

**Cost reporting cadence** — 8 options

`Daily hot costs` · `Weekly cost report` · `Bi-weekly (prep and post)` · `Per-episode report` · `Season-to-date report` · `Monthly (post-production)` · `Ad hoc on financier request` · `Final cost report`

**Corporate vehicle** — 9 options

`Single-purpose LLC` · `Limited partnership` · `C-corporation` · `UK Film Production Company (FPC)` · `Irish Designated Activity Company for Section 481` · `Hungarian or Czech SPV` · `Paired co-production SPVs under a treaty` · `Loan-out corporation (for individual talent)` · `Icelandic parent with a US operating arm`

**Waterfall positions** — 16 options

`Collection account manager's fee` · `Residuals and guild payments` · `Sales agent commission` · `Sales agent recoupable expenses` · `Senior bank principal, interest and fees` · `Tax credit lender` · `Gap lender` · `Mezzanine / super-gap` · `Completion guarantor's advanced funds` · `Equity recoupment at 100%` · `Equity premium to 110-125%` · `Deferments (first position)` · `Deferments (behind equity)` · `Producer corridor` · `Net profit participants` · `Gross participants (paid from first dollar, off the top)`

**What a producer does when the picture goes over** — 20 options

`Cut scenes from the remaining schedule` · `Combine scenes or locations` · `Move a location to stage or backlot` · `Drop a day and redistribute the work` · `Add a day and fund it from contingency` · `Work a sixth day (paid at premium)` · `Split off a second unit` · `Reduce background headcount` · `Reduce or defer VFX shots` · `Descope a build to a partial set` · `Renegotiate a vendor mid-shoot` · `Ask cast or department heads to defer` · `Draw contingency` · `Request additional financing from equity` · `Call on the bond` · `Trigger the studio's overage obligation` · `Sweep savings from closed accounts` · `Push cost into post and hope` · `Shut down and restructure` · `Abandon the picture`

**Cash flow drawdown mechanism** — 8 options

`Weekly draw against a certified cost report` · `Monthly draw` · `Milestone draws (close, start of photography, wrap, delivery)` · `Single advance at close` · `Revolving facility` · `Letter of credit` · `Escrow release on conditions` · `Reimbursement in arrears`

**Deferral types** — 10 options

`Full fee deferral` · `Partial fee deferral` · `Deferred to first position (ahead of equity)` · `Deferred behind equity recoupment` · `Contingent deferment (paid only on a defined event)` · `Conversion of fee to backend points` · `Facility or stage deferral` · `Lab, post or VFX house deferral` · `Vendor deferral against a delivery payment` · `Deferred against a specific territory's receipts`

**Collection account manager** — 7 options

`Fintage House` · `Freeway Entertainment` · `Compact Collections` · `Bank-administered escrow` · `Sales-agent-held account` · `Producer-held account (no CAM)` · `Studio as collection point`

**Budgeting and accounting software** — 9 options

`Movie Magic Budgeting (Entertainment Partners)` · `Showbiz Budgeting` · `Hot Budget` · `Excel` · `Vista (EP)` · `Smart Accounting (Cast & Crew)` · `GreenSlate` · `Sargent-Disc / Cast & Crew UK` · `Studio ERP (SAP or equivalent)`

**Payroll service** — 8 options

`Entertainment Partners` · `Cast & Crew` · `GreenSlate` · `Media Services` · `Wrapbook` · `Sargent-Disc (UK)` · `The Team Companies` · `In-house payroll`

**Insurance in the package** — 19 options

`Cast insurance` · `Negative / faulty stock, camera and processing` · `Props, sets and wardrobe` · `Extra expense` · `Miscellaneous equipment` · `Third-party property damage` · `Commercial general liability` · `Auto liability and physical damage` · `Workers' compensation` · `Umbrella / excess liability` · `Errors & Omissions` · `Weather cover` · `Political risk (foreign shoots)` · `Drone / UAV` · `Aviation and watercraft` · `Animal mortality` · `Union travel accident` · `Communicable disease (usually excluded or heavily restricted)` · `Foreign package for an overseas unit`

**Television deal shape** — 9 options

`Network licence fee plus studio deficit` · `Streamer cost-plus commission (full budget plus a markup, all rights)` · `Full studio ownership and finance` · `International co-production` · `Pre-buy or distribution advance` · `Format licence` · `AVOD/FAST licence` · `Tax-credit-led independent series` · `Branded or sponsored content`

**Above-the-line compensation shape** — 16 options

`Fixed fee, pay-or-play` · `Scale` · `Scale plus 10 (agent's commission on top)` · `Overscale` · `Fee plus deferment` · `Fee plus box office bonus` · `Gross participation from first dollar` · `Adjusted gross participation` · `Net profits / MAGR participation` · `Backend buyout for a fixed premium` · `Streaming success bonus` · `Per-episode fee with a 10-out-of-13 guarantee` · `Per-episode fee with a 13-out-of-13 guarantee` · `Holding fee between seasons` · `Annual escalators across option years` · `Most-favoured-nation cast terms`

**Television amortization method** — 7 options

`Straight-line across the ordered episodes` · `Weighted by actual usage per episode` · `Front-loaded to early episodes` · `Pilot amortized separately from the series` · `Standing sets amortized across a single season` · `Standing sets amortized across multiple seasons` · `Fully expensed to episode one`

**Lot and facility charge model** — 9 options

`Flat stage day or weekly rate` · `Four-wall (bare stage, bring everything)` · `Production services agreement (bundled crew, equipment and space)` · `Overhead as a percentage of budget` · `Actual cost plus markup` · `Deferred against distribution` · `Equity in the picture in exchange for facilities` · `Term deal for a returning series` · `Discounted rate to fill occupancy`

### What gets got wrong

- Fringes are not a percentage of the budget. They are percentages of specific wage accounts, with annual ceilings. Employer OASDI at 6.2% stops at $184,500 of an individual's wages in 2026 (verified, IRS Topic 751), so the effective fringe rate on a high-paid individual FALLS across a long engagement. A flat fringe multiplier is the single most common way a film budget model becomes fake, and a 22-episode television order is where it breaks worst.
- Contingency and the bond's strike price are two different numbers and the whole bond mechanic depends on the gap between them. The strike price is the budget LESS contingency, bond fee, financing costs, publicity and named exclusions. The guarantor's money only attaches above the strike price — meaning the producer's contingency is spent first, entirely, before the bond does anything. Model one number and the bond is a label.
- The bond guarantees DELIVERY, not the budget, and not the film. A guarantor that takes over will finish a deliverable picture as cheaply as it can. It has no interest in it being good. A takeover that does not visibly degrade the finished film has not been modelled.
- Abandonment is a real and rational option. If cost-to-complete exceeds what the financiers advanced, the guarantor repays them and the picture is never finished. Every model that assumes a bonded picture always gets delivered is wrong.
- Money is committed when the purchase order is issued, not when the invoice arrives. Cost = actuals + commitments + estimate to complete. A system that tracks only invoices will report a picture on budget right up to the week the paper lands, which is exactly how real productions get surprised.
- The interesting number in a cost report is the TREND, not the variance. A picture $200k over and improving is healthy; a picture $50k over and deteriorating for three straight weeks is in trouble. Store the series, not the snapshot.
- Days behind is the best leading indicator of final cost, and it is available a week before the cost report shows anything. Half a day behind on day 5 of 40 is recoverable and cheap; half a day behind on day 38 is unrecoverable and gets paid for in overtime, a sixth day, or a cut scene.
- Tax credits are earned on qualified spend in a jurisdiction, not on the budget. Above-the-line salaries of non-residents are commonly excluded or capped, the audit disallows things, and the money arrives months to years after wrap — California 4.0 verified: refundable, non-transferable except for independent films, $750M per year, credit on the first $120M of qualified spend on a feature, $1M minimum budget. The lag is why credits get borrowed against, and the borrowing is a real budget line.
- Georgia is verified as 20% base plus a 10% Georgia Entertainment Promotion uplift, $500k minimum annual spend, no annual cap, no sunset, mandatory audit since 1 January 2023 — and it is TRANSFERABLE, not refundable, which means it is sold at a discount to face rather than paid out. Transferable and refundable are different financial instruments and should not share a code path.
- Section 181's federal expensing election and the interaction with bonus depreciation under the 2025 tax act were NOT verifiable in this session (web search budget exhausted). Treat any US federal film tax expensing figure as unverified and check the current statute before putting a number in the engine.
- Guild pension and health percentages were NOT verifiable in this session. SAG-AFTRA, DGA and WGA employer contribution rates are all in the high teens to low twenties as percentages of covered earnings, and IATSE contributions to the Motion Picture Industry Pension & Health Plans are structured differently again — hourly dollar amounts for health and defined-benefit pension plus a percentage-of-gross individual account contribution. Model the STRUCTURE (percentage versus hourly versus hybrid) from this note and get the live numbers from the current agreements before shipping.
- Cash flow failure kills pictures that are on budget. If the money is drawn in arrears against certified cost reports and the production has no working capital, Friday payroll can fail in week three of a perfectly budgeted shoot. Cash position must be simulated separately from budget position.
- Savings are not automatically reusable. Whether the producer keeps an underspend or the studio sweeps it is a policy, and under a sweep regime the rational producer hides savings against future exposure. That behaviour is modellable and it is what actually happens.
- Overages are not one thing. A schedule overage (behind on days), a rate overage (paid more per unit than budgeted) and a scope overage (the picture got bigger) have different causes and different cures, and applying the wrong cure spends money without fixing anything. If the engine has one 'overage' number it cannot support a good decision.
- Cost per episode is meaningless without the amortization method. The same season reports wildly different per-episode costs depending on whether the pilot, standing sets and showrunner are spread straight-line, weighted, or expensed to episode one — and a back-nine pickup changes the denominator, which changes the reported cost of episodes already shot.
- Cross-boarded television days belong to more than one episode, so a shooting day's cost must be split by an explicit key (scene count, page count, or negotiated). Every per-episode number downstream — cost report, licence-fee comparison, incentive claim — depends on a split rule most models never implement.
- Streaming cost-plus and network deficit financing are different games, not different numbers. Cost-plus pays the whole budget plus a markup and takes all rights, so there is no deficit, no backend and no library upside — the optimal play is throughput. Deficit financing loses money for years and can pay hugely in syndication — the optimal play is survival to season four.
- Pay-or-play means the money is gone whether or not the picture is made. Commitments made during development are liabilities that survive the picture's death, and a production company that pays out on three collapsed projects has a real balance-sheet problem with nothing to show.
- Residuals and pension contributions survive delivery and run for the life of the copyright. They follow the picture through assignments and are a standing liability, and in television they are a common reason an old show becomes too expensive to keep on a service.
- E&O insurance is a delivery item, and a gap in the chain of title blocks it. No E&O means no delivery, no delivery means no minimum guarantee, no MG means the bank loan accrues interest against nothing. A missing signature from four years ago can cost a finished picture its financing.
